Abstract: Generating dynamically feasible motions for legged and manipulating robots requires solving a large, nonlinear, non-convex optimal control problem (OCP) in which contact wrenches must satisfy friction-cone constraints throughout the motion, not only at isolated instants. Existing solvers are sensitive to the initial guess and scale poorly with the number of joints. This paper introduces LEAP, which transcribes the OCP with pseudospectral collocation and, instead of a Newton or SQP solve, integrates a primal--dual gradient flow of a Powell--Hestenes--Rockafellar augmented Lagrangian to compute a solution. Because the flow is stiff, this paper designs a linearly implicit integrator with a Gauss--Newton metric, momentum, and a merit-based step controller that keeps the augmented Lagrangian monotone. Across locomotion benchmarks on five robot platforms, from planar hoppers to 32-joint humanoids, LEAP is the only method to solve every tested problem, achieves substantial speedups over Crocoddyl and Aligator on several benchmarks, and reaches trackable references at smaller compute budgets. The resulting trajectories are validated by tracking them in a high-fidelity simulator and on hardware. We will make the code public upon final acceptance of this paper.

</details>

Abstract: Accurate trajectory tracking is essential for autonomous underwater vehicles operating with uncertain hydrodynamics and limited thruster authority. This paper develops a low-complexity adaptive prescribed performance controller for uncertain underwater vehicle dynamics with coupled and saturated inputs. The proposed method adapts the performance functions directly through the mismatch between the requested and saturated control inputs, enlarging the performance funnels only when the available actuation cannot meet the nominal specifications. A coupled invariant-set analysis establishes boundedness of all closed-loop signals, prescribed bounds on the filtered tracking errors, and explicit feasibility conditions. Comparative simulations on a BlueROV2 underwater vehicle show that the adaptive design maintains prescribed performance during a figure-eight maneuver, whereas conventional prescribed performance control loses feasibility under input limits. A real-time pool experiment further demonstrates stable trajectory tracking and the practical implementation of the proposed method on an underwater robotic platform.

</details>

Abstract: Autonomous mobile robots in warehouses and production facilities operate in precisely the environments that defeat point-feature visual odometry: featureless aisles, uniform walls, repetitive racking and specular floors. Such environments are, however, rich in straight-edge structure: rack uprights, door frames, wall junctions and cabinet outlines present high-contrast linear edges stable across wide viewpoint changes. A line-feature extension to cuVSLAM, a CUDA-accelerated visual-SLAM system, is presented that recovers this signal. Lines are parameterised as Plücker coordinates for geometric transport and as the minimal 4-DOF orthonormal representation of Bartoli and Sturm for optimisation; a Cauchy-robust point-to-line residual with analytic Jacobians is folded directly into cuVSLAM's per-frame Gauss-Newton pose solve, and the same line state is jointly refined in the Schur-complement sliding-window bundle adjustment. An adaptive information weight causes lines to assume more of the estimation burden exactly as point features become scarce, and the extension adds residual blocks to existing solvers rather than introducing a second estimator, reducing exactly to the unmodified point pipeline when disabled. Evaluated in RGB-D mode under SE(3) Umeyama alignment with no scale correction, line features reduce mean absolute trajectory error by 38.5% over the 22 sequences of the TUM RGB-D Freiburg3 collection and by 30.1% over the 8 Replica room and office sequences, where median translation error falls by 38.9% and median rotation error by 66.4%, with zero tracking failures in any configuration tested.

</details>

Abstract: Executing heavy-duty physical manipulation in unpredictable environments remains a major challenge for quadrupedal robots lacking dedicated manipulators. This paper presents SOMA, a hierarchical reinforcement learning framework for whole-body non-prehensile manipulation of multi-object stacks using a quadruped robot. Unlike traditional methods restricted to two-dimensional planar pushing, SOMA integrates explicit robot base-height control, unlocking full three-dimensional pushing strategies. This capability enables the quadruped to manipulate complex payload stacks as a unified system while preventing relative sliding or tipping of upper layers. The architecture factorizes control into a high-level manipulation policy using a Simplified Structured State Space Sequence (S5) encoder to reason over long-horizon temporal history and predict bounded target tracking commands via Beta distributions, and a low-level locomotion policy that dynamically adapts leg stance configurations. In extensive simulation benchmarks, SOMA achieves a $90.3\%$ success rate for single-object reposing and up to $90.1\%$ for dynamic two-box stacks under stringent error margins ($0.05\text{ m}$ position error, $1^\circ$ orientation error). Finally, we validate zero-shot sim-to-real transfer on a Unitree Go1 quadruped robot across diverse payload masses and geometries, demonstrating active base-height adjustments to maintain traction and balance during heavy pushing tasks.

</details>

Abstract: Slip-induced falls when riding motorcycles are difficult to avoid even for expert riders and represent a major cause of single-vehicle accidents. Despite their prevalence, no practical solution has yet been proposed to suppress this phenomenon. We previously introduced a motorcycle model applicable to systems with both front- and rear- wheel steering. In this paper, we extend that model and formulate the suppression of slip-induced falls as an optimization problem that distributes assistive control inputs according to vehicle speed and road surface conditions. To evaluate the proposed method, we developed a remotely operated motorcycle and conducted experiments under two scenarios: slalom driving on a low-friction surface at 5.5 m/s, and turning maneuvers on synthetic ice to simulate driving over manholes on rainy days. The results demonstrate that our approach successfully suppresses falls under these challenging conditions.

</details>

Abstract: Safe autonomous navigation using monocular vision remains challenging because explicit obstacle states are not directly observable. This letter introduces a visual safety-certification paradigm that uses scale-free, depth-normalized relative motion encoded by monocular optical flow as the representation for collision-risk certification, without reconstructing obstacle position, velocity, geometry, or a metric world model. Unlike conventional optical-flow-based navigation approaches that rely on image motion to shape avoidance behavior, the proposed framework directly encodes collision-cone geometry and safety constraints in this relative-motion representation. An online Concurrent Learning observer estimates the normalized relative velocity and provides a computable estimation-error bound. This uncertainty is propagated into the collision cone through angular inflation and a monocular robot-footprint margin, yielding a Robust Vision-Based Collision Cone Control Barrier Function (RV-C$^3$BF). An optimal-decay CBF quadratic program minimally modifies the nominal control input to enforce the resulting robust safety constraint under the stated assumptions. Extensive simulation and real-world experiments on a TurtleBot3 in cluttered environments with static and dynamic obstacles demonstrate collision avoidance using only monocular vision and onboard proprioception, with the estimation-error bound containing the measured error in $98.3\%$ of simulation samples and the robot achieving collision-free navigation in $93$ of $100$ real-world trials.

</details>

Abstract: A wind-derived heading can guide a robot towards an odour source even after the wind reverses, but its reliability depends on the plume's transport history. On a quadruped initially facing the source, holding the initial heading reached it in every reversing-wind trial, whereas comparison controllers never settled there. An exact geometric condition links heading correctness to net odour transport, and simulations separate sensing error from departures from nominal flow. Where the alternating wind directions are too close for reversal alone to produce wrong-side headings, the wrong-side rate rises from 1.0% under uniform transport to 4.7% in a stochastic flow. Re-acquiring headings from new odour contact improves simulated search when contact is intermittent. These results identify distinct requirements for wind-guided search: a heading supported by the transport geometry and sufficient intermittent contact to replace it.

</details>

Abstract: Passive human videos of everyday activity offer robots access to a massive amount of human experience without requiring a demonstration for each task. However, using this experience requires recovering knowledge from unstructured video recordings and adapting it to a different body (i.e., the robot). To overcome these challenges, we present mobile code-as-policy (MoCaP), an agentic framework that turns passive human video recordings into guidance for an end-to-end mobile manipulation policy. MoCaP extracts spatial memory and cues of how people prepare to interact with objects, which inform navigation and the search for a manipulation-ready configuration. Guided by passive human experience and current observations, MoCaP uses a closed-loop code-as-policy to coordinate planning and tool use primitives to solve motions under the robot's geometry and kinematic constraints. On a real mobile manipulator, MoCaP achieves 80% success on each of two mobile manipulation tasks in an indoor environment whereas existing approaches cannot succeed at the task. Video-derived spatial memory improves object-navigation success from 70% to 100%, and human approach prior raises the rate of finding a reachable base pose from 0% to 100% at an unfavorable start location. Videos and code are available on the project website: https://mocap-7.github.io/MoCAP-page/.

</details>

Abstract: Contact-rich robotic tasks require coordinated motion and impedance under uncertain interaction dynamics and physical constraints. While recent methods on learned policies provide useful interaction strategies, they may fail when contact conditions differ from those encountered during training. Model-based generative methods show promising receding-horizon trajectories optimization results, but often lack structured behavioral guidance and refinement that jointly accounts for constraint geometry and actual contact responses. To this end, we propose Manifold Generative Annealing (MGA), a joint model-free and model-based generative framework for motion--impedance optimization. MGA first organizes pretrained reinforcement learning policies into structured sequence proposals, retaining task-specific interaction strategies without committing to their execution. It then evaluates these proposals alongside exploratory samples through model-based rollouts for refinement or separate execution selection. Crucially, realization-aware manifold refinement aligns updates from the refinement bank with both task constraints and locally achievable physical responses, allowing learned experience to guide online adaptation under changing contact conditions. Experiments cover surface scanning, peg insertion, and humanoid pushing, with improved safety success rate of impedance control under nominal and sensing-shift conditions.

</details>

Abstract: Continuous physical human-robot interaction requires robots to rapidly adapt their control behaviour without interrupting task execution. However, existing ROS 2 control architectures rely on stop-to-switch mechanisms that introduce substantial delays and interrupt execution. We present an open-source, sub-millisecond reflex engine based on an enhanced ROS 2-compatible multimode controller that enables deterministic switching between control modalities and integrates reflex behaviours for mitigating contact hazards and kinematic limit violations. Experiments on a Franka Robotics manipulator demonstrate switching delays below 0.1 ms, more than four orders of magnitude faster than conventional ros2_control, while maintaining continuous operation during safety-critical events. In a multi-stage manipulation task, the proposed framework reduced execution time by 3.5% and peak trajectory jerk by more than threefold. By eliminating runtime middleware overhead, controller transitions are implemented as lock-free pointer operations, enabling reflex-level responsiveness within an open-source robotics framework.

</details>


### [384] [Perceptual Comparisons of Viscoelastic Renderings: Fractional- vs Integer-Order Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21965)
*Gorkem Gemalmaz,Harun Tolasa,Volkan Patoglu*

Abstract: Realistic haptic rendering of viscoelastic materials requires reproducing time-dependent behaviors such as creep and stress relaxation, which arise from the material's inherent memory effects. Fractional-order viscoelastic models have been shown to capture these dynamics accurately and compactly, but it remains unclear whether their increased fidelity translates into perceptual benefits during haptic interaction. In this work, we investigate whether users perceive fractional-order models as more realistic than conventional integer-order models while rendering viscoelastic materials. We compare the fractional-order standard linear solid (FO-SLS) model with two integer-order alternatives: the 3-parameter SLS model and the 5-parameter generalized Maxwell model. We consider two complementary approaches to optimal parameter selection to evaluate rendering realism from both objective and perceptual perspectives: In the first approach, we determine the model parameters via least-squares based system identification of physical viscoelastic reference materials to establish evaluation baselines. In the second approach, we determine population-level perceptually optimal parameters using a qualitative-feedback-based human-in-the-loop optimization and aggregation method. This method establishes model parameters that maximize perceived realism of viscoelastic behaviors through qualitative evaluations. Human-subject experiments indicate that the FO-SLS model is perceived as significantly more realistic than both integer-order alternatives under both parameter selection approaches. Overall, this study provides experimental evidence that fractional-order viscoelastic models are not only theoretically well-motivated, but also perceptually relevant for realistic haptic rendering of creep- and relaxation-dominated behaviors of viscoelastic materials.

</details>

Abstract: Training humanoid whole-body vision-language-action (VLA) policies requires costly robot demonstrations with aligned robot-view observations and controller-compatible whole-body state-action labels. Egocentric human demonstrations offer scalable supervision but have misaligned views and sparse motion cues. Existing approaches either pretrain policies that still require robot data for adaptation or generate controller-specific commands that limit reuse. Conversion also requires robot-view observations aligned with the generated whole-body motion. We present KODA (Kinematics-Observation Data Alignment), which converts egocentric RGB demonstrations into whole-body VLA training data using a reusable canonical trajectory to guide robot-view synthesis and generate controller-specific whole-body state-action supervision. KODA first generates a simulator-checked whole-body trajectory using a sparse tracker with sampling-based command optimization. This generated trajectory directly determines the onboard camera poses used to synthesize corresponding robot-view observations. The motion, observations, and task instruction form a canonical record. For each deployment controller, KODA optimizes commands to track the stored motion and records the corresponding action labels and proprioceptive states. When switching controllers, KODA retains this canonical record and regenerates whole-body state-action supervision for co-training with matching robot demonstrations. We evaluate KODA on four Unitree G1 loco-manipulation tasks using two whole-body controllers, SONIC and HEFT. With robot data held fixed, macro-average success on conditions covered by human demonstrations but absent from robot data improves by 45.0 and 46.25 percentage points, respectively. Under both controllers, this metric shows an upward trend across the evaluated human-data budgets, suggesting potential for further scaling.

</details>


### [386] [When to Ask for Help: A Decision-Theoretic Account and Measurement Standard for Slow-Module Scheduling in Dual-System Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21160)
*Shilin Zhang*

Abstract: Dual-system vision-language-action (VLA) models pair a high-frequency fast policy with a slow, expensive module, and published trigger mechanisms for invoking it lack a shared objective, measured costs, and any standard separating schedule quality from module quality. We provide the missing account: invocation is formalized as a calibrated decision problem whose fixed-ratio, fixed-budget, and history-gated schedules are degenerate cases of one quantity, the Value of Intervention (VoI); a renewal baseline proves the optimal schedule periodic under state-independent collapse, with adaptive gains governed by the cost ratio $ρ$ and the dispersion of collapse. Measured systems span three orders of magnitude in $ρ$ (FiS-VLA $0.95$; RoboICL about $10^3$), and a direct test confirms the low-$ρ$ prediction: calibrated gains vanish under homogeneous collapse and grow with heterogeneity. Fourteen closed-loop arms on LIBERO test the contested middle of the continuum ($ρ=1.53$): the adaptive trigger allocates its queries exactly as the threshold rule predicts, yet the language-rewrite channel cannot convert them into success on either suite, and a revert actuator that undoes a failed rewrite is the only intervention with zero net downside on both LIBERO-Spatial and LIBERO-Goal, where the rewrite deficit deepens from thirteen to forty-two points. A four-measure leak-free protocol cuts apparent early-warning AUC from $0.93$ to $0.62$ on $929$ deployment traces, a detection-limited frontier theorem converts detection quality into a schedulability statement, and a compilation loop turns measured value functions into per-task refresh periods within $2.4\%$ of the closed-loop oracle. Together these results turn slow-module invocation from a design opinion into a measured decision.

</details>

Abstract: Current humanoid dexterous manipulation remains largely confined to end-effectors and simple grasps, falling short of humans' ability to engage the entire body by bracing and sliding bulky objects against the arms and torso. We introduce Whole-Body Dexterity (WBD), a general framework that uses the robot's body and appendages as contact surfaces to grasp and manipulate objects. Our sim-to-real pipeline first generates a dataset of feasible whole-body grasps, then trains reinforcement-learning experts on a general any-to-any repositioning objective by sampling initial and goal grasps from the dataset, and distills these experts into a single policy that uses proprioception and object geometry. We show that the distilled policy transfers zero-shot to a real Unitree G1, where it repositions a basketball and a cardboard box to regions around the torso and completes a long-horizon task that stows a box under one arm, carries it from a shelf to a table, and places it. The controller operates interactively, steered by coarse motion references from a human teleoperator. Across real-world whole-body manipulation tasks, the distilled controller completes 90% of trials, compared with 18% for direct retargeting and 4% for a generalist whole-body controller.

</details>


### [388] [Evo-DexBench: Benchmarking Generalization across Related Tasks for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21180)
*Bing Cheng,Yiran Mao,Zewei Ye,Yifan Jin,Xinyi Chen,Ruifeng Tu,Shuhuai Wang,Tao Lin,Zhimu Zhou,Yuxin Du,Fangwei Zhong,Junchi Yan,Bo Zhao*

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: General-purpose dexterous manipulation requires policies to transfer knowledge from related tasks to new tasks with only a few target demonstrations. Existing benchmarks often provide too many target demonstrations. They also leave the differences between source and target tasks uncontrolled. As a result, they cannot clearly distinguish knowledge transfer from task memorization. Our key insight is the Core-Gen principle. Core tasks are related tasks that share a functional interaction and provide source knowledge, while a Gen task is a novel target within the same functional family that has only a few demonstrations. This setup tests whether a policy can reuse functional interaction knowledge. Based on this principle, we propose Evo-DexBench, which controls both source-task relatedness and target-task exposure. It comprises 30 tasks in six functional families and a VR teleoperation pipeline. The resulting dataset covers three everyday scenes per task. It contains 1,248 multimodal demonstrations collected with simulated Shadow Hands equipped with 123 taxels. Experiments show that even the best-performing method achieves an average success rate of only 38.5%, highlighting the challenges existing methods face in transferring functional interaction knowledge. Further analysis shows that, across the two tasks, joint Core+Gen training improves performance by 12% and 38%, respectively, over training on Core followed by fine-tuning on Gen. This indicates that joint training makes better use of Core knowledge and that functionally matched Core data yields greater gains in success rate than mismatched data. We release the Evo-DexBench dataset and benchmark to support the evaluation of dexterous manipulation policies' generalization across related tasks.

</details>


### [389] [LIN-SLAM: Lightweight LiDAR--Inertial Neural SLAM for Real-Time Onboard Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21199)

Abstract: Geometric light detection and ranging (LiDAR)--inertial simultaneous localization and mapping (SLAM) provides efficient state estimation but lacks the continuous map representation and global refinement capabilities of neural implicit SLAM, while neural implicit methods remain computationally expensive. We propose Lightweight LiDAR--Inertial Neural SLAM (LIN-SLAM), a hybrid method that couples high-rate geometric LiDAR--inertial estimation with neural implicit tracking, continual mapping, and global refinement for accurate real-time onboard SLAM. Experiments on twelve representative sequences from three datasets spanning 32-, 64-, and 128-channel LiDAR configurations show that LIN-SLAM achieves the lowest overall mean absolute trajectory error (ATE) among the evaluated state-of-the-art geometric and neural implicit methods, while substantially reducing runtime compared with existing neural implicit SLAM methods. On a Unitree Go2 equipped with an NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX and a 16-channel LiDAR, LIN-SLAM sustains real-time localization and neural mapping over a 1.47-km indoor trajectory through challenging repetitive corridor environments, with an endpoint closure error of only 0.141~m. To the best of our knowledge, LIN-SLAM is the first LiDAR--inertial SLAM system with a neural implicit backend to achieve real-time online operation on a mobile robotic platform while maintaining state-of-the-art localization accuracy. The code will be released upon acceptance.

</details>


### [390] [An Agentic Framework for Proactive Robots with Procedural Assistance Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21211)
*Dohyun Lee,Minho Park,Jeonghoon Park,Dongyoon Hwang,Junha Hyung,Sungwon Hwang,Byungkun Lee,Jaegul Choo*

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Proactive robots should anticipate user needs and provide assistance before being explicitly asked. A straightforward approach is to infer human intentions from commonsense knowledge, but the same observed behavior can imply different needs across users, potentially leading to unwanted assistance. We observe that everyday routines in the real world exhibit recurring patterns, so learning these patterns can reduce ambiguity in proactive decisions. Based on this, we propose a memory-based agentic framework in which a VLM task predictor reads a Procedural Assistance Memory (PAM) alongside the current observation to decide when to assist and what task to perform, and a VLA executor performs the predicted task. PAM is structured as a finite-state machine over activity phases, per-state assistance rules, and event-driven transitions; a reflection VLM revises PAM from each day's interaction trace without weight updates or retraining. In our 5-day desk-assistance evaluation, memory-free baselines remain conservative, with limited recall on proactive tasks. Our system improves consistently with accumulated experience, achieving the highest precision, recall, and F1 and reducing first-fire delay from approximately 5 seconds on Day 1 to 1 second by Day 5. Our approach also adapts to changes in the user's routine through continued memory reflection, recovering from memory--routine misalignment after a single memory update. The project page is available at https://davian-robotics.github.io/PAM/

</details>

Abstract: Artificial microbots capable of precise and autonomous navigation could provide versatile platforms for microscale transport, manipulation, and task execution in complex environments. However, reliable navigation becomes increasingly challenging at the micrometre scale, particularly when the surrounding environment changes dynamically. Here, we develop a vision-guided closed-loop navigation framework for light-driven cube-shaped hematite/TiO2 Janus microbots with a characteristic size of approximately 3 μm. Real-time visual localization using a custom-trained YOLOv11 object-detection model is integrated with discretized waypoint-based magnetic steering to enable precise navigation along complex prescribed trajectories, with more than 90% of the measured trajectory deviations remaining below 6 μm across the tested path geometries. The framework further enables arbitrary user-defined free-hand trajectories and autonomous navigation through a physically confined maze. In dynamically changing environments, A* path planning enables real-time path replanning while avoiding multiple independently swimming 5 μm spherical Janus micromotors. Finally, the microbot selectively captures multiple cargo particles and autonomously transports and delivers them to a predefined location while simultaneously avoiding dynamic obstacles. These results establish an integrated platform combining programmable trajectory control, autonomous path planning, real-time environmental adaptation, and task execution within a single light-driven Janus microbot system.

</details>


### [392] [CLIS-MPPI: Closed-Loop Model Predictive Path Integral Control With Initial-State Sampling Under Execution Delay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21239)
*Zidong Liu,Xu Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 解析错误


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Sampling-based motion planning such as Model Predictive Path Integral (MPPI) control has become attractive for robotics due to its ability to handle nonlinear dynamics and non-smooth objectives without gradient information, while leveraging parallel rollout computation for real-time performance. However, in real-world robotic systems, the optimized control input is often applied after a non-negligible planning delay, causing a mismatch between the state used in forward simulation and the actual state at control execution, which can significantly degrade planning accuracy and may even lead to unsafe behavior such as collisions. This paper proposes Closed-Loop MPPI with Initial-State Sampling (CLIS-MPPI), which explicitly addresses execution-time state mismatch by modeling planning delay as a distribution over plausible execution-time initial states, performing rollout optimization from sampled initial states, and applying a rollout-based local feedback correction during control execution. The proposed method is validated through real-world experiments on a 6-DoF UR5e manipulator in a narrow-passage task involving obstacle avoidance and end-effector target reaching. Across 5 real-world runs of the same task, standard MPPI collided in 2 runs, whereas CLIS-MPPI remained collision-free in all 5 runs. Averaged over all collision-free runs for each method (3 for MPPI and 5 for CLIS-MPPI), CLIS-MPPI reduced the average task completion time from 25.4s to 24.4s and the average end-effector path length from 0.456 m to 0.425 m. It also reduced the overall joint-position and joint-velocity root mean square errors (RMSEs) from 0.100 rad and 0.108 rad/s to 0.017 rad and 0.011 rad/s, respectively. These results demonstrate improved safety, planning-execution consistency, and motion smoothness.

</details>


### [393] [LICOA: Learning Instruction-Conditioned Optical Actions for Mobile Spatial Augmented Reality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21282)

Abstract: Spatial augmented reality (SAR) augments physical environments with projected content, but most systems assume that the projector pose, target region, or motion path has been fixed or specified. We instead make projected light an embodied action: given a language instruction and current visual observations, a system must decide how to move and where and at what scale to place the projection. We introduce LICOA to realize this capability. Its differential-drive chassis provides room-scale approach and repositioning, while a steerable mirror with two degrees of freedom (2DoF) and projection scale provide rapid local control of the beam. A shared interface maps synchronized external and onboard RGB cameras with robot control state to physical optical actions across pretrained action models. Because instruction-conditioned optical action lacks dedicated training data and a common evaluation protocol, we construct a matched simulation embodiment and a corpus with 150 tasks and 7,500 rendered episodes, then evaluate models trained only on this corpus in previously unseen real-world environments. Across 480 complete closed-loop real-world execution trials (rollouts) on 12 tasks, the best adapted action model achieves a 57.50% success rate and 40.64% success weighted by path length, compared with 8.33% and 5.15% for the same architecture trained from scratch. A further 150 real-world ablation rollouts support the need for both camera views and coordinated control of the chassis and mirror. These results establish a physical platform, shared perception and optical action interface, corpus, and real-world evaluation protocol for treating projected light as an embodied action.

</details>


### [394] [MR-MPCC: Model Predictive Contouring Control for Robotic Manipulator Tracking under Moving Reference Frames](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21292)

Abstract: Maintaining small path-tracking errors under moving reference frames is essential for robotic manipulation tasks with tight execution tolerances, such as machining and surgical automation. Conventional time-indexed controllers, including tracking Model Predictive Control (MPC), rely on a fixed temporal correspondence with the reference trajectory and may therefore accumulate path-relative error under abrupt reference-frame motion or timing mismatch. This paper presents MR-MPCC, a Model Predictive Contouring Control (MPCC) framework for velocity-controlled robotic manipulators with 6-DoF end-effector pose regulation under Moving-Reference disturbances. A hierarchical control architecture is adopted, in which an upper-level MPCC optimizes path progress while penalizing contouring and orientation errors, and a constrained resolved motion rate control (RMRC) layer maps the commanded Cartesian twist to feasible joint velocities under joint-level and safety constraints. MR-MPCC is implemented in a ROS2--MuJoCo pipeline and validated on a UR5e platform under moving-reference disturbances, where the path frame is perturbed and tracked by electromagnetic sensors. Compared with PI control and time-indexed tracking MPC under matched disturbance, MR-MPCC achieves lower geometric path-following error and smaller deviations in the moving path frame, demonstrating improved path-following performance under reference-frame motion.

</details>

Abstract: Efficient synthesis of contact-rich manipulation trajectories is essential for downstream robot learning. Gradient-based planning offers a promising approach, but jointly optimizing motion and contact decisions remains challenging. Meanwhile, many existing planners run on CPUs with limited throughput. We present CQDC-Warp, a GPU-accelerated differentiable quasi-dynamic simulator for batched model predictive control (MPC). We use it to study contact-guided trajectory optimization, in which intended robot--object contacts are specified before planning and encouraged through soft costs, while the simulated dynamics determine contact geometry and frictional response without a prescribed contact-mode schedule. Across $17{,}408$ trials on the $17$-object Allegro in-hand rotation benchmark, our framework achieves $79.8\%$ success, compared with $73.7\%$ for CFree and $65.2\%$ for IMPACT, and reduces mean trajectory-generation time by factors of $27.0$ and $50.2$, respectively. On a single NVIDIA RTX 4090 with $2{,}048$ parallel worlds, forward and backward throughput reach $2.06\times10^5$ and $2.25\times10^5$ world-steps per second, respectively. Further experiments cover manipulation with LEAP and Shadow hands, environmental contact exploitation, bimanual manipulation, whole-body control, and real-world trajectory replay on a Sharpa hand.

</details>


### [396] [A Black-Box Positioning Solution for Effortless Integration in Wearable Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21353)
*Anh-Van Vu,Changmin Sung,Dongsoo Han*

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Accurate localization is critical for a wide range of applications, including navigation, augmented reality, safety management, and healthcare. However, achieving accurate positioning remains challenging, especially in GPS-degraded environments. Pedestrian Dead Reckoning (PDR), which estimates position based on inertial sensor data, offers a promising alternative but typically demands substantial effort in algorithm design and system integration. This complexity often leads to prolonged development timelines and elevated costs. To address these challenges, we present the PDR Sensor (PDRS), a plug-and-play, black-box PDR module optimized for seamless integration into wearable and IoT platforms. PDRS integrates a microprocessor and an inertial measurement unit (IMU) within a compact, low-power module. It executes real-time sensor fusion onboard, providing position updates via standard interfaces (I2C, SPI, UART). Experimental evaluations demonstrate its high robustness, achieving a step detection error of just 0.16% over 1250 steps and a traveled distance error of 1.87% under diverse conditions. The system further exhibits low latency (2.5 ms) and modest power consumption (52.35 mA). Unlike conventional studies, the PDRS leverages a holistic co-design of hardware and software to enhance performance and integration aspects, simplifying adoption without requiring deep expertise. By lowering technical barriers, PDRS enables developers and researchers to prioritize innovation in IoT and wearable technologies, accelerating prototyping, reducing development time, and advancing industry progress. Future work aims to enhance accuracy, reduce power usage and module size, and integrate AI-driven techniques to better mitigate drift.

</details>


### [397] [Hierarchical Building-Floo-Room Framework for Privacy-Preserving Autonomous Delivery in Large-Scale Buildings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21361)

Abstract: Autonomous delivery in large scale buildings requires coordinated planning across multiple spatial levels, including building, floor, and room, while ensuring scheduling efficiency. Existing methods often focus solely on algorithmic development, neglecting integration with physical robotic platforms, and rarely validate performance in real world scenarios. Many approaches also overlook hierarchical building structures and room level privacy constraints, limiting practical applicability. To address these challenges, this paper presents a hierarchical delivery framework based on a Building-Floor-Room abstraction, which decomposes the problem across multiple levels to significantly reduce optimization complexity. We formulate a building aware extension of the capacitated vehicle routing problem (CVRP) that explicitly models inter building and inter floor connectivity, room level access constraints, and privacy sensitive areas, enabling globally feasible and privacy compliant task assignment and routing. Based on this hierarchical model, the framework can evaluate delivery efficiency across different building layouts and optimize depot placement for specific configurations. At the execution level, we introduce a novel hardware design for non interactive, privacy preserving delivery, supported by reachability aware distances and a closed loop mapping, localization, and navigation system, ensuring consistency between planned routes and physical navigation. The proposed framework is validated on a real multi sensor robotic platform, demonstrating reliable multi floor delivery with improved path rationality and overall efficiency.

</details>

Abstract: Robotic manipulation often requires remembering interactions that are no longer visible. We introduce Latent Transition Memory (LTM), which stores history as compact latent transitions between consecutive observations. Instantiated on $π_{0.5}$ as LTM-$π$, our method uses a frozen latent action encoder, a lightweight adapter, and causal KV caching for incremental inference without memory-specific semantic annotations or external reasoning-model calls. With DreamDojo transitions, LTM-$π$ achieves 58.54% success on RoboMME and 80.5% on LIBERO-Mem, performing best among baselines without external reasoning models or memory-specific annotations. Ablations support the contribution of transition content beyond temporal cues and the efficiency of delayed memory admission. Scaling RoboMME demonstrations tenfold improves success from 57.67% to 87.50%, while a Cup Swap diagnostic highlights the role of process supervision in temporal composition. Under a 200-token memory budget, success remains 84.00%, versus 87.50% without compression. Finally, real-robot counting success improves from 11.25% to 82.50%. Together, these results demonstrate the effectiveness, scalability, and efficiency of latent transition memory, highlighting its capacity for complex temporal information, bounded-context operation, and real-world robotic control.

</details>


### [399] [Fast-VLA: You Only Think Once for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21389)

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies commonly place a multi-billion-parameter model inside the feedback loop, repeatedly recomputing task context whenever they predict a new action chunk. Yet task understanding changes far more slowly than the robot state. We introduce Fast-VLA, which invokes a frozen 4B VLM once per episode to contextualize the instruction and initial views as fixed, multi-layer key-value task memory. During execution, a lightweight online actor fuses learned time- and arm-indexed queries with current visual and proprioceptive feedback to form action queries. These queries retrieve task context from the fixed memory and predict action chunks without re-running the foundation model. Fast-VLA achieves 92.46% and 92.40% success on RoboTwin 2.0 Clean and Randomized, and 95.95% and 79.11% on LIBERO and LIBERO-Plus evaluation. Against three continually conditioned baselines on the same hardware, it reduces RoboTwin amortized policy inference time by 10.9-17.1x and requires 1.22 GiB of post-prefill online resident memory. Task-memory swaps further show that the actor relies on episode-matched task memory. These results show that task context can be established once while closed-loop control remains responsive throughout execution. Project page: https://garfield-kh.github.io/FastVLA

</details>

Abstract: Sound-Triggered Mobile Manipulation (STMM) requires robots to respond to sound events by navigating to the associated objects and performing appropriate physical interactions. Existing STMM evaluations primarily vary object layouts within limited architectural structures, leaving challenges to unseen scenes insufficiently explored. We introduce Multi-scene Sound-Triggered Mobile Manipulation (MuSTMM), a dataset that combines spatial audio rendering and physical interaction across 31 scenes with diverse architectures and object layouts. MuSTMM contains 14.36 million precomputed binaural Room Impulse Responses (RIRs) and a scene-disjoint split for evaluating single-event and multi-event settings in unseen scenes. We further augment the Navigation Policy Model with explicit spatial memory that complements acoustic cues with observed geometry. Specifically, we accumulate pose-aligned depth observations in a shared coordinate frame and project this memory into an egocentric Bird's-Eye View (BEV) occupancy map. Fusing map features with sound context produces a goal descriptor that provides both target-location and scene-structure priors, guiding navigation to the target region required for subsequent physical interaction. This policy achieves a 2.60% success rate boost over existing methods, while component ablations further support the effectiveness of spatial memory. MuSTMM and the navigation policy provide a testbed for the new challenge of studying sound-triggered mobile manipulation in unseen scenes. The code and dataset will be released upon acceptance.

</details>

Abstract: Rigid closed chains can admit indeterminate internal reactions even when their motion is fully specified. This paper develops a modular inverse-dynamics workflow that combines path-based motion reconstruction with recovery of uniquely determined actuator and joint-wrench outputs. Analytical second-order differentiation of assembled transformation paths supplies passive velocities and accelerations on a regular closure branch. Spatial Newton--Euler balances are then assembled over the physical body-and-joint graph. Established nullspace and dual criteria identify and recover unique outputs, while balance, ideal-joint admissibility and affine-family diagnostics assess nonunique reference reactions. Evaluation against Simscape Multibody uses prescribed motion of a seven-degree-of-freedom heavy-duty hybrid serial--parallel manipulator under common rigid-body, ideal-joint assumptions. The results show close agreement with the reference for passive positions, actuator efforts and uniquely recoverable constraint-wrench components. Reaction diagnostics support compatibility with the admissible numerical load family.

</details>


### [402] [Policy-Aligned Predictive Verification for Adaptive VLA Action-Chunk Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21434)

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies generate action sequences that reduce repeated inference, but deciding how much of each sequence to execute is critical to task success. Existing adaptive methods make this decision using generation stability or prediction agreement, yet these signals do not establish whether executing a candidate prefix preserves the ability to complete the task. We introduce a policy-aligned predictive verifier (PAV) that anticipates the consequences of candidate action prefixes and evaluates their compatibility with successful continuation before execution. Rather than using future prediction to generate actions, our method evaluates existing proposals in the VLA's internal task-conditioned representation space. To learn local judgments from terminal task outcomes, we combine trajectory-level survival supervision with a localization prior that preserves useful behavior within failed rollouts. The resulting validity scores guide execution-horizon selection. Evaluations with pi_0 and pi_0.5 across LIBERO, RoboTwin 2.0, and real-world manipulation show improved success-query trade-offs. These findings point toward a different role for future prediction in robotics, using it to assess how far to trust a policy's proposed actions rather than to generate new ones.

</details>


### [403] [X-Fly: Learning to Reach and Stop in Full-Chunk Aerial Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21450)

Abstract: Aerial vision-language navigation (VLN) agents built on large vision-language models are expensive to query. Executing a complete predicted action chunk before replanning amortizes this cost, reducing model inference from 195 to 42 ms per executed waypoint for our policy. It also moves the states at which the policy is queried away from expert demonstrations, which supervise neither recovery from these states nor termination at the learner's own arrival. On OpenFly test-seen, no-stop failures account for 47.78% of full-chunk behavior cloning (BC) outcomes. We present X-Fly, a staged training framework for full-chunk aerial VLN. Recovery DAgger labels learner-visited decision states with smooth chunks that reconnect to future expert segments and with STOP at the first in-region state. Flow-GRPO then jointly refines the chunked flow controller and a Bernoulli STOP policy from same-task closed-loop outcomes, supplemented by outcome-verified stopping supervision. With eight controls per decision, X-Fly reaches 74.18% SR on test-seen and 51.73% on test-unseen, compared with 16.45% and 18.43% for full-chunk BC and 32.04% and 29.46% for DreamFly.

</details>


### [404] [CarryOn: A Dual-Loop Harness for Personalized and Reliable Robot Assistance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21453)
*Zixuan Wang,Yuqi Liu,Yuxin Chen,Sengqiao Yang,Jinhui Ye,Pengguang Chen,Changsheng Lu,Shu Liu,Bei Yu,Jiaya Jia*

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: While generalist vision-language-action (VLA) models have enabled diverse open-world manipulation, real-world robotic assistance requires two critical capabilities they currently lack: personalization and reliable execution. First, executing an instruction like "make me a sandwich" requires grounding subtask decomposition in persistent user preferences rather than relying on a single generic solution. Second, reliable deployment demands explainable safety assurance that proactively prevents hazards and gracefully recovers from occasional physical failures. To address these gaps, we present CarryOn, a dual-loop harness designed to augment a frozen VLA policy. The planning loop maintains persistent user-level and scene-level context that is continually grounded against new observations and revised through interaction, enabling personalized task selection and execution grounding across conversations and changing scenes. The execution loop actively supervises policy rollouts, shielding actions from predicted hazardous contact. When execution fails, the system invokes reusable control-based recovery skills that restore task progress and are refined through reflection on earlier failures. On a real robot and in simulation, CarryOn dispatches preference-consistent subtasks across users and scenes, reduces the violation rate of an already safety-finetuned VLA from 52.6% to 6.1%, and increases recovery success from 13% to 80% when refined skills are reused.

</details>

Abstract: In endovascular interventions, physicians rely on planar DSA and X-ray fluoroscopy to interpret vessel anatomy and device motion, yet converting these images into feedback for autonomous navigation remains difficult. A perception and control method is formulated for two-dimensional image feedback. Vessel masks are converted into a labeled centerline graph, and device-tip estimates are projected onto a planned path to obtain progress, deviation, and direction. A rule-based controller uses these quantities for delivery, branch selection, probing, rollback, and recovery. The method was evaluated on a physical dual-device robotic platform in 65 closed-loop runs using simulator-rendered planar images across three paths. All runs reached at least 0.95 maximum observed guidewire progress; two runs (3.1%) met the prespecified failure rule of three or more autonomous stop events. The results establish route-level performance on the tested platform; clinical image sequences and broader anatomy remain to be evaluated.

</details>

Abstract: Reliable worker localization is essential for real-time industrial safety management, but seamless outdoor-indoor positioning remains challenging. GNSS performs well outdoors yet degrades near buildings and fails indoors, while many indoor systems require costly infrastructure or extensive calibration. This paper presents a practical safety-oriented positioning system that uses resource-constrained wearable tags and a device-cloud split architecture for continuous outdoor-indoor worker tracking. The tag integrates GNSS, IMU, barometric sensing, LTE Cat-M communication, and performs lightweight sensor fusion, while the cloud server executes map-aware fusion for continuous tracking. Developed as an operational platform, the system also includes a database, a map tool, and a dashboard that provide real-time monitoring, geofencing-based access alerts, and safety and productivity analysis. Experiments with multiple participants, each walking an approximately 1400 m outdoor-indoor route, using LTE Cat-M communication, achieve mean errors of 1.32 m indoors and 3.30 m outdoors, while maintaining low positioning latency and efficient power consumption. These results demonstrate the feasibility of wearable-based seamless positioning for scalable real-time safety management.

</details>

Abstract: Language-conditioned humanoid motion generation enables users to create and refine robot movements through natural language. The central challenge is to connect an underspecified request to concrete motion structure that can subsequently be revised relative to an accepted reference. We present TRACE, a generation and editing framework for the Unitree G1 humanoid robot built around typed, measurable kinematic quantities. Our insight is to ground generation conditions in paired training motions and editing goals in the current motion. A language model learns to predict support, root, and hand events from motion-derived supervision; their time-aligned encodings complement the original text in a diffusion generator. Gravity-referenced states and trajectory--contact objectives improve kinematic consistency. For editing, typed requests define body--time scopes and source-relative goals. A separate reference-conditioned diffusion editor uses these goals and revised events to realize changes, while sampling-time projection preserves protected state coordinates. Quantitative evaluation and human assessment show improved semantic alignment and motion quality over the compared methods. Controlled ablations support the value of motion-grounded conditioning and kinematic consistency, while multi-turn editing evaluations demonstrate more reliable execution of successive relative requests under shared preservation constraints. Together, these results support typed kinematic structure as a common basis for motion generation and reference-aware revision.

</details>

Abstract: Specialized vision-language-action (VLA) policies acquire strong domain-specific skills, yet consolidating these skills into one shared policy remains challenging. On-policy distillation (OPD) transfers teacher knowledge on inputs induced by the student, addressing the distribution mismatch encountered during deployment. For flow-based VLAs, this mismatch arises at two levels: environment observations and intermediate action-generation states. We propose a two-level on-policy distillation framework that couples student-driven environment interaction with teacher velocity supervision along student-generated action flow trajectories. Frozen domain specialists jointly supervise one student. After each update, the student collects new environment experience and generates fresh action-flow trajectories. Starting from a joint supervised fine-tuning (SFT) initialization, Velocity Loss OPD yields absolute success-rate gains of 22.55% on LIBERO-40, 33.4% on CALVIN five-instruction sequences, and 22.6% on Meta-World MT50. Compared with Action Loss OPD and Teacher-rollout SFT, it achieves absolute success-rate gains of 6.4-9.5% on LIBERO-40, 9.7-18.3% on CALVIN, and 3.2-4.2% on Meta-World MT50. Analysis by sequence length further shows larger success-rate gains on longer CALVIN sequences. These results demonstrate effective multi-domain capability consolidation through two-level on-policy supervision, with a single student deployed across domains.

</details>

Abstract: Assist-as-needed (AAN) control must distinguish between changes in intended motion and changes in effective voluntary torque capability, even though both can produce similar kinematic and interaction responses. A dual-measurand multimodal MHE-MPC architecture is developed as the base framework, in which the intended trajectory and the task-dependent effective voluntary torque capability are estimated online as distinct quantities, and both estimates are used to adapt robotic assistance. Building on this architecture, the ambiguity between the two update channels is formulated as a continuous credit-assignment problem and resolved by an attribution gate. A trajectory-change indicator is obtained from persistent corrective interaction, whereas a capability-change indicator is derived from the residual between normalized muscle activation and dynamics-reconstructed voluntary torque. Nine healthy participants completed factorial target-height-load trials and a pre-post fatigue cup-transport task. Without target-height input, the gated method generated correctly ordered Low-Middle-High references under every load: target changes were preferentially assigned to trajectory adaptation, whereas added load shifted adaptation toward the capability channel. It achieved the lowest RMSE in all nine conditions (0.017 rad versus 0.041 rad for ungated dual estimation and 0.127 rad for fixed-reference error-based AAN). Post-fatigue capability estimates decreased and assistance increased at all heights, supporting functional feasibility.

</details>


### [410] [AVALON: Zero-Shot Indoor Navigation with Human-Seeded Topological Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21586)

Abstract: Indoor service robots often enter unmapped buildings whose layouts and task constraints are already familiar to users. We address this human-familiar, robot-unseen setting with AVALON, a topological navigation framework organized around human-seeded layered spatial memory, node-verified topological execution, and task-triggered, evidence-gated human--robot memory co-updating. The robot has no prior on-site traversal or site-specific training. Deployment requires only a floor plan, place clues, and an initial topology-odometry alignment. Visual depth point-goal navigation leverages verified node arrivals to perform translational re-anchoring, without requiring a robot-constructed global SLAM map. Human--robot memory co-updating fuses complementary sources of information. Human operators specify task constraints that are unobservable via vision and report offline environmental modifications, whereas the robot provides physical-state evidence through active in-situ validation. Task relevance governs whether human-reported environmental changes undergo robot validation during the active mission. Field-collected perceptual evidence gates memory updates, maintaining an explicit separation between human-specified permissions and robot-observed traversability. Our experiments on a Unitree Go2 show that AVALON with X-NavDP completes a 12.81 m Intersection-to-Dining mission in 46.4 s (5/5) with no mapping, 40.9% less robot time than DimOS-Go2 (78.5 s, 4/5), while policy-only X-NavDP times out in all of the 5 runs. These demonstrate the superiority of the proposed topological navigation framework.

</details>

Abstract: While Assist-as-Needed (AAN) control aims to maximize user participation in robot-assisted training, existing methods relying solely on physical metrics cannot distinguish muscular inability from mental disengagement, leading to suboptimal interventions. To address this ambiguity, a Flow-theoretic multimodal AAN framework is proposed, fusing EEG-based mental engagement and sEMG-based physical engagement into an adaptive impedance control strategy. A four-quadrant fuzzy decision engine translates these dual physiological metrics into continuous control policies. This enables the upper-limb exoskeleton to inject feedforward assistance during intent-execution mismatch (e.g., motor output attenuation despite mental focus) and escalate guiding stiffness to penalize mental distraction. The framework was validated with healthy participants against a state-of-practice sEMG-only AAN baseline. Kinematically and mechanically, the proposed framework reduced robotic energy expenditure by 71.9\% while maintaining precise trajectory tracking, confirming it elicited greater voluntary human effort. Physiologically, the system increased the time spent in the neuroplasticity zone (Flow State Ratio) from 4.6% to 42.8%. These results validate that decoupling and jointly regulating mental and physical states enhances the selectivity of robotic assistance, mitigating user passivity and sustaining the engagement crucial for motor learning.

</details>


### [412] [Training-Free Target-Aware Refinement of 3D Gaussian Language Map](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21619)
*Sibaek Lee,Kyeongsu Kang,Seongbo Ha,Yewon Jeon,Joonyeol Choi,Daegeol Ko,Jaehyuk Hur,Taemin Choi,Minsu Kim,Hyungjoo Ahn,Wonsok Yoo,Hyeonwoo Yu*

Abstract: Distilling vision-language features into 3D Gaussian Splatting yields a language map that can be queried with arbitrary text. In deployment, however, a small set of user-specified targets must be reliably located, which current distilled maps often miss, while open-vocabulary queryability for other words must remain intact. We propose a training-free, target-aware refinement of an already-distilled language map. Guided by a text-promptable segmenter, the visual embedding inside each detected target region is blended with the target text embedding, a convex combination whose coefficient sets the amount of injected prior, while non-target regions are left untouched. Injecting too much overwrites appearance cues and general queryability, so we seek, for each target, the smallest coefficient that still lets it win, and we obtain every coefficient in closed form, leaving no hyperparameters to tune. We further identify a failure mode of injection in language-goal localization. Text embeddings share a common component, and injecting them makes the standard cosine read-out query-agnostic, which a centering step in the read-out removes. Across three benchmarks, the refinement consistently improves open-vocabulary segmentation, roughly doubling the base mIoU on Replica, and localizes language goals more reliably than the base map. It also transfers as a plug-in to off-the-shelf field-based and object-level language maps and supports a real-world demonstration in which a legged robot navigates to natural-language goals. Project page and code: https://lab-of-ai-and-robotics.github.io/target-aware-langmap/

</details>

Abstract: Recent Vision-Language Models (VLMs) enable automated preference labeling for robotic manipulation, yet their deployment remains impractical since each query incurs substantial inference cost, while VLMs frequently abstain on visually challenging comparisons. We propose SPEAR, a framework that transforms VLMs into efficient preference oracles via two synergistic mechanisms. First, we design distinguishability-aware query selection to reduce abstention rates by targeting only informative and distinguishable comparisons. Second, we deploy an adaptive reward transition that bootstraps exploration with VLM-synthesized guidance before preference-based refinement. Experiments on the manipulation benchmarks demonstrate that SPEAR achieves 88.9% average success, outperforming baselines by 23.3 percentage points, while significantly reducing abstention and wall-clock training time on contact-rich tasks. SPEAR learns three Mobile ALOHA manipulation tasks fully online on real hardware, demonstrating end-to-end real-world applicability. By addressing query efficiency and exploration challenges, SPEAR establishes VLMs as practical preference oracles for scalable robot learning without manual reward engineering.

</details>

Abstract: Small observation or action errors can compound during closed-loop execution and disrupt a trained imitation learning policy. We study whether the original expert demonstrations can improve robustness through test-time hidden-state steering without additional demonstrations or policy retraining. Our analysis of a trained policy finds that expert hidden-state variation is concentrated in relatively few dimensions, while the directions capturing this variation differ across regions. Motivated by this geometry, we propose Diffeomorphic Hidden-State Steering (DHS), which learns an invertible nonlinear coordinate map from expert hidden states obtained by replaying the demonstrations through the frozen policy. DHS attenuates coordinates with low expert variance more strongly than those with high variance, then maps the modified coordinates back to hidden-state space. This allows steering directions to vary with the current hidden state while retaining every coordinate. Across four simulated manipulation tasks, DHS improves mean success under observation noise over the unmodified Transformer policies. Controlled comparisons yield higher mean success with learned nonlinear coordinates than with linear coordinates under both hard projection and full-rank rescaling. Combining DHS with action smoothing improves mean success rate under observation noise in certain tasks compared with action smoothing alone.

</details>


### [415] [MAIA: Anticipating Interaction Anomalies in Contact-Rich Manipulation via Multimodal World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21686)

Abstract: Reliable contact-rich manipulation requires detecting execution deviations early enough for corrective action. Yet most monitoring methods are reactive, identifying anomalies only after they become observable. Anticipating such anomalies is challenging because visual observations only partially reveal physical interaction, while planned actions describe intended motion rather than their actual physical consequences. We address this problem with MAIA, a multimodal framework for anomaly anticipation. Its vision-action-force world model learns nominal interaction dynamics and predicts the visual and force responses of planned actions, while a lightweight anomaly head predicts impending interaction anomalies. To enable systematic evaluation, we introduce CRIA, a real-world multimodal dataset of 2,852 trajectories spanning 18 contact-rich manipulation tasks, with frame-level annotations of anomaly onset and evolution. Experiments on both seen and novel tasks show that MAIA achieves more accurate anomaly discrimination than representative baselines while providing earlier warnings, with bACC scores of 0.765 and 0.710 and median anticipation lead times of 28.0 and 25.1 steps, respectively. Moreover, using these predictions for anomaly-aware action selection improves end-to-end task success from 52.6% to 61.5% without retraining the underlying manipulation policy. These results highlight the value of modeling future physical consequences for proactive anomaly anticipation.

</details>


### [416] [Uni-PNP: Unified Prehensile and Non-prehensile Policy Learning for Generalizable Object Pose Reaching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21735)

Abstract: Object manipulation often requires combining non-prehensile actions with grasping. However, existing approaches typically develop these capabilities separately and integrate them by sequencing distinct primitives through prescribed intermediate objectives, limiting the coordination of contact motions and grasping. Their integration also remains sensitive to object and gripper geometry and depends on particular environmental supports, complicating real-world deployment. We present Uni-PNP, a unified policy that jointly learns prehensile and non-prehensile manipulation for object pose reaching across diverse objects and grippers. To achieve this, we first pretrain a geometry encoder to predict contact-induced object motion using a contact-dynamics dataset constructed from simulated interactions between diverse objects and grippers. We then train the policy in a two-stage reinforcement learning curriculum from graspable objects to ungraspable peers, encouraging the discovery of non-prehensile interactions and their integration with grasping. Experiments show 76.39% success on hundreds of seen objects with grippers, and 57.91% on unseen peers, outperforming the strongest pose-reaching baseline by 20.52 and 17.79 percentage points, respectively. We also analyze emergent behaviors, including nonprehensile and prehensile repositioning, grasp acquisition that requires grasping after tipping, and table-assisted reorientation while grasping, and transfer to the real-world scenario. Project page available at lgswdn.github.io/Uni-PNP.

</details>


### [417] [Comfortably Stuck: Evaluation Pitfalls in Bench2Drive's Closed-Loop Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21741)
*Thomas Schwarzfischer,Emad Aghajanzadeh,Moritz Thoma,Nael Fasfous,Manoj Rohit Vemparala,Pierpaolo Mori,Daniel Mueller-Gritschneder,Ulf Schlichtmann*

Abstract: Closed-loop evaluation is widely regarded as a more faithful way to assess end-to-end (E2E) driving models than open-loop evaluation, and Bench2Drive, built on the CARLA simulator, has become the dominant benchmark for it. We show that its evaluation protocol itself contains pitfalls that can misrepresent the models scored on it. Bench2Drive's comfort metric, adapted from nuPlan, has several implementation errors and a segmentation scheme that rewards standing still, so it mostly measures how long a vehicle stays stationary, correlating with stationary time at r = 0.97 across eight models. We propose a corrected, distance-based metric that cuts that correlation to r = 0.06. Under it, the strong driving-score/comfort tradeoff the original metric reports is no longer detectable once models are compared at matched driving speed, which suggests that comfort trades against how fast a model drives rather than against how well it drives. We also uncover two pitfalls in how the harness supplies navigation signals: the default controller can replace a model's own steering with an angle toward the route target point, and the target point supplied at evaluation time can differ substantially from what a model saw in training. We demonstrate both on UniAD and VAD, where their effects differ sharply, and argue that future closed-loop evaluations should check for them. We will release all fixes as standalone code, so corrected comfort scores can be obtained from logged trajectories without re-running closed-loop simulation.

</details>

Abstract: Imitation learning enables robots to perform complex tasks by leveraging human-collected demonstrations. However, most existing approaches require labor-intensive data collection with additional teleoperation systems, which hinders scalability. Human videos represent a natural source of data that implicitly contain knowledge of manipulation behaviors. However, transferring them to robots remains challenging due to the visual domain gap and the absence of explicit action labels. In this work, we introduce TDV, a trajectory-based framework that leverages data generation to enable generalizable manipulation. TDV focuses on task-relevant objects by leveraging plug-and-play 6D pose estimation techniques to extract object trajectories from human videos, thereby mitigating the impact of irrelevant backgrounds and varying viewpoints. As a modality-agnostic intermediate representation, trajectories are inherently independent of the visual domains and different embodiments, enabling efficient data generation. Our method outperforms previous approaches on RLBench simulation tasks, achieving near 100\% success rates across seven tasks. In real-world experiments, TDV enables cross-embodiment generalization manipulation from one-shot human demonstrations by leveraging efficient trajectory generation techniques.

</details>


### [419] [Cross-Embodiment Causal Transfer from Human Videos to Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21798)

Abstract: Scaling robot manipulation models requires large amounts of interaction data, yet collecting diverse real-robot experience remains costly. Human manipulation videos, in contrast, provide abundant and diverse task experience that could potentially benefit robot learning. However, differences in morphology, action spaces, and control mechanisms make human motions unsuitable as direct supervision for robot actions, raising a scientific question: what knowledge in human videos should be transferred across embodiments to robot manipulation? To address this question, we analyze subtask transfer through the lens of causal beams, focusing on which contextual conditions and interaction dependencies should be retained to support local task effects. Based on this perspective, we propose Causal-Beam-Guided Subtask Transfer (CBST). CBST learns manipulation-related representations from human videos and grounds them in task-level semantic variables using robot demonstrations. Within a predefined manipulation model, causal-beam constraints guide contextual information selection by requiring retained assignments to sustain local mechanism outputs while preserving nontrivial responsiveness to retained inputs. The selected semantic information conditions a world-action model, allowing the robot to generate actions from its own observations without requiring human-robot motion correspondence. Experiments on robot manipulation benchmarks show improvements in task success over the corresponding world-action model baselines.

</details>


### [420] [Time-Optimal Ergodic Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21823)

Abstract: Retrieval tasks are a form of search task where a robot must explore, find an object, and return with it as quickly as possible. Recent work in ergodic control has demonstrated time-optimal path planning for search tasks where visitation is proportionally matched, within some tolerance, to a given target distribution. However, these planners minimize the duration of a full deterministic search, while retrieval missions end their search at an unknown point in the trajectory and must return to a given base. In this work, we propose a new formulation of the time-optimal ergodic search problem that models detection as a Poisson process and minimizes total mission time by including the return phase in the objective. Experimental results show that time-optimal ergodic retrieval (TOER) achieves up to $19\%$ shorter mission durations relative to ergodic baselines and that this advantage grows as the return phase becomes more costly. Simulation and hardware implementations validate TOER's compatibility with environments of non-convex geometry as well as both 2D and 3D search domains.

</details>

Abstract: Solving optimal control problems for high-dimensional mechanical systems subject to constraints remains challenging due to nonlinear dynamics and non-convex feasible sets. This paper introduces MAGMA(Multiplier-Augmented Geometric Motion Algorithm), an Affine Geometric Heat Flow (AGHF)-based trajectory optimizer that incorporates an augmented Lagrangian framework solved via a primal-dual flow. A theoretical characterization of the primal-dual AGHF is provided, establishing that MAGMA is a gradient flow on an appropriately defined energy. MAGMA is validated across a variety of challenging trajectory generation problems with obstacles, state limits, and actuation limits. Comparative evaluations against state-of-the-art solvers demonstrate that MAGMA achieves high quality solutions with significantly reduced tuning effort on the hardest problems.

</details>


### [422] [What Should Robot Policies Learn to Predict? Rethinking Supervision Targets in Vision-Language-Action Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21915)
*Haitao Shen,Hanyu Liu,Yihan Lin,Huiping Liu,Junbo Cui,Daoheng Li,Yang Li,Jing Zhang,Wenxin Li,Yuan Yao*

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies are typically trained with action prediction as the primary supervision signal, since robots ultimately execute actions. Auxiliary objectives such as latent-action (LA) and future-vision (FV) prediction are often added to improve policy learning, yet they compete with action prediction for the same training budget. We ask whether, under a fixed budget, allocating a substantial share of training samples to such objectives, rather than to further action prediction, can improve policy learning. We build a unified VLA framework that couples a two-stream multimodal diffusion transformer (MMDiT) with a sample-level router, which assigns each training sample to either action prediction or an auxiliary objective and thus makes supervision allocation explicit. Within this framework, we instantiate three LA targets based on LAPA and its DINO-feature variants, and three FV targets based on DINOv3, V-JEPA 2, and Wan2.2 representations. Across LIBERO, CALVIN, and four real-robot tasks, suitable LA and FV supervision outperforms action-only training under the same budget, even when action prediction receives no more than half of the training samples. FV generally outperforms LA, although the strongest LA target remains competitive on CALVIN. Diagnostic analyses suggest that DINO-based LA targets emphasize relative motion, whereas FV targets emphasize absolute position and task identity. We therefore argue that what robot policies learn to predict, and how training is allocated among these targets, should be treated as explicit design choices rather than defaulting to action-dominant training.

</details>


### [423] [TS-WAM: Temporal Latent Actions and World-Action Coupling for Vision-Language Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21922)

Abstract: Many vision-language-action policies rely on action-labeled robot demonstrations and lack an explicit mechanism for predicting future visual states. These limitations hinder learning from action-free videos and can encourage overfitting to demonstration conditions, weakening generalization and robustness. We present TS-WAM, a lightweight Teacher-Student World-Action Model that addresses these challenges through self-supervised temporal learning and explicit future prediction. Its vlm-jepa framework learns from heterogeneous data, including robot demonstrations and action-free human videos, using an EMA target branch without requiring a separately trained external teacher. By coupling vlm-jepa with delta-lwm, TS-WAM learns to predict latent actions from the current observation and instruction and uses them to predict future visual features that guide action generation at inference. A unified end-to-end training architecture jointly optimizes temporal representation learning, visual dynamics, and robot control without a separate latent-action or world-model pretraining stage. The EMA target branch is removed at inference, keeping the deployed policy lightweight. Experiments on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and SimplerEnv, together with real-world manipulation experiments on Franka Research 3 and RevoTron, demonstrate that TS-WAM achieves strong manipulation performance, generalization, and robustness.

</details>

Abstract: Growing interest in in-space servicing, manufacturing, and assembly has renewed attention on reliable metal joining for modular construction and on-orbit repair. Robotic welding is promising, but reduced-gravity and low-pressure operation remains poorly characterised and flight-like tests are limited. The UK Space Agency-supported ISPARK project is developing a physics-grounded digital twin for structured process de-risking. This paper evaluates a model-based prototype combining a finite-volume thermal weld-pool model, Gaussian-process (GP) and neural-network surrogates, and a simulated six-axis manipulator. The reduced model includes solid-liquid phase change, effective thermocapillary transport, and a local-pressure parameter. Gravity is assessed separately through a hydrostatic-to-capillary pressure ratio and does not enter the thermal energy equation. Across repeated five-fold cross-validation, the GP has lower normalised mean absolute error than the neural network for all eight outputs. In 10 held-out boundary cases, 74 of 80 solver responses fall within nominal 90% GP predictive intervals. A three-output GP query takes 249 microseconds, including scaling and predictive variance. In a simulated UR5 L-shaped-seam study, a proportional controller adjusts the synergic power-source setting as travel speed falls near the corner. Finite-volume re-evaluation finds all 12 closed-loop operating points and 9 of 12 open-loop points within an illustrative 1.0-3.0 mm root-side-width band. These simulation results support using the framework to screen welding conditions and control strategies before experimental tests. Thermal-vacuum, targeted reduced-gravity, and robotic hardware-in-the-loop studies are needed to assess the model and controller.

</details>
