Abstract: Biological visual navigation achieves remarkable robustness at extremely low power, whereas frame-based robotic systems relying on artificial neural networks remain limited in both respects, particularly for energy-constrained micro aerial vehicles (MAVs) operating in complex environments. Inspired by the event-driven, sparse, and asynchronous principles of biological visual systems, neuromorphic sensing and computation provide a promising alternative for robust and energy-efficient navigation. In this paper, we propose the first end-to-end event-driven neuromorphic paradigm for MAV visual navigation in complex environments by integrating neuromorphic vision, spiking neural networks, and neuromorphic processors. The proposed method first extracts the environmental representation from event streams using a spiking perceptual representation module. This representation is fed into a spiking navigation module, where guidance learning and population coding enable efficient policy learning with few time steps. The navigation module then outputs a waypoint converted into executable trajectory points. Experiments show that the proposed neuromorphic method achieves navigation performance comparable to ANN-based methods while markedly reducing power consumption. The spiking perceptual module consumes only 19.57% of the power of its ANN counterpart, while the spiking navigation module deployed on Intel Loihi consumes only 12.37% of that required by its ANN counterpart running on NVIDIA Orin NX. Beyond prior neuromorphic systems focused mainly on relatively simple navigation subtasks, we demonstrate a complete neuromorphic visual navigation system spanning simulation and real-world deployment. Our results establish the feasibility of the neuromorphic paradigm for autonomous navigation and provide a bio-inspired pathway toward robust and low-power MAV navigation.

</details>

Abstract: Autonomous driving planning requires selecting trajectories that remain safe, rule-compliant, and comfortable under complex multi-agent interactions, yet robust closed-loop decision making in planning-critical scenes remains challenging. Existing diffusion- and RL-based planners often rely on generic rewards and use relational structure mainly for representation, limiting how planning-critical rollouts shape policy improvement. We present GRID-Planner, a graph--reward integrated diffusion planning framework that aligns generative planning with structured relational signals. Specifically, GRID-Planner combines graph-aware importance weighting, a time-indexed relational reward surrogate, and an advantage-weighted diffusion objective with on-policy self-distillation, forming a rollout-return-advantage-update loop without explicit likelihoods. Experiments on nuPlan and interPlan show that, compared with the diffusion-only baseline, GRID-Planner reduces collision rate, lane deviation, and jerk by 35.8\%, 60.8\%, and 27.4\%, respectively, and improves the planning score by 3.4\%. Project website: https://github.com/yangzhaojason/GRID-Planner.

</details>

Abstract: Flow-based Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies are inherently stochastic: different noise seeds can produce action chunks of varying quality from the same observation. At test time, however, the policy typically commits to a single sample without an explicit estimate of its quality. In fine-grained manipulation, executing a suboptimal action can irreversibly disrupt task progress and lead to failure. To exploit policy stochasticity, we introduce a training-free two-axis trigger that uses two initial samples to measure within-chunk temporal variation and cross-sample variance, detecting the critical states. Once triggered, a JEPA-based latent world model ranks candidate action chunks by predicting action-conditioned future latent states and the corresponding changes in task progress. By operating in latent space, the verifier avoids pixel reconstruction and enables efficient and more accurate ranking. Across three real-world tasks, $\textit{i.e.,}$ pen insertion, pipetting, and apple placement), our method raises the average completion score from 50.8% to 65.5%, while adding 326 ms for sampling and verification per triggered decision on a single RTX 4090. Consistent gains are also observed in simulation, where the average success rate rises from 66.4% to 73.6% in the clean setting of RoboTwin 2.0, from 62.8% to 69.6% in its randomized setting, and from 96.9% to 97.6% on LIBERO.

</details>

Abstract: Embodied Visual Tracking (EVT) is an essential capability for intelligent robots in human-robot collaboration, requiring an agent to follow a designated target person in dynamic environments relying solely on egocentric visual perception. However, persistent pedestrian occlusions in dynamic scenes pose critical challenges to existing EVT methods. Lacking proactive visibility preservation, reactive tracking methods frequently experience prolonged target loss under severe occlusions. Therefore, we propose VP-EVT, a framework that treats target visibility as an explicit objective of motion control rather than a by-product of following. Acting on this objective requires understanding both what obstructs the target and how robot motion can change that obstruction. VP-EVT therefore uses language-guided perception to extract separate target and pedestrian observations. When occlusion makes current observations incomplete, VP-EVT combines visual history with ego-motion to maintain estimates of their relative positions and motion. Using these estimates and the current following waypoints, VP-EVT predicts whether continued following will sustain the obstruction. When the predicted risk is low, the original following motion is retained; otherwise, a recurrent controller combines updated estimates with maneuver context from preceding decisions to coordinate viewpoint adjustment and the return to stable following. Moreover, we introduce OccEVT-Bench for closed-loop evaluation of persistent occlusion and collect expert demonstrations for training. On its Risk split, VP-EVT improves success rate by 121.4% relative to a competitive baseline and reduces visibility deficit and longest target-loss duration by 78.8% and 74.4%, respectively.

</details>

Abstract: Learning from demonstration is an effective strategy for endowing robots with manipulation capabilities but requires training on datasets comprised of massive amounts of demonstrations. Existing demonstration collection approaches either require constant human involvement, for example, via teleoperation or are specialized for a single task. Task and Motion Planning (TAMP) systems are able to autonomously solve and therefore generate demonstrations for general-purpose tasks; however, learning from their demonstrations has proven difficult due to multi-modality in their solutions. In this work, we build on recent work leveraging GPU acceleration for TAMP and propose DemoStream, a TAMP system that optimizes demonstration quality in order to improve behavior cloning success rates. First, we introduce batched TAMP sampling operations along with a new TAMP algorithm that correspondingly performs batched constraint satisfaction in order to explore many solutions in parallel. We conduct simulation experiments ablating different action spaces, cost functions, and batch sizes to analyze the effect of these choices on downstream policy success rates. Finally, we show that we can leverage the safety guarantees of TAMP to directly collect real-world demonstrations at an average rate of 66 demos per hour. See our website for policy rollout videos: demostream.github.io.

</details>


### [430] [Plans Are Not Executions: Adapting Frozen World-Model Planners to Controller-Induced Execution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22041)

Abstract: World-model planners evaluate candidate trajectories by predicting their future consequences, implicitly relying on the plan-to-motion relationship learned during training. At deployment, a different downstream controller may execute the same planned trajectory differently, creating a planning-execution gap between the frozen model's imagined future and actual execution. A natural remedy is to replace the planned trajectory with its predicted or observed execution. However, we find that better execution fidelity does not necessarily yield better decisions: directly substituting predicted execution trajectories yields only a 0.048 PDMS improvement across 10 unseen controller configurations, far smaller than the gain from our proposed adaptation. This suggests a semantic mismatch between a planned trajectory, which serves as a reference for downstream control, and an executed trajectory, which already describes the resulting post-controller motion.
  We introduce ExecutionBridge, which infers controller-specific execution characteristics from planned-executed trajectory pairs and uses them to adapt the native ego latent rollout while keeping the original planned trajectory unchanged. The planner, world model, and reward head remain frozen. On NAVSIM, ExecutionBridge consistently improves planning across unseen controller configurations and multiple frozen world-model planners, with substantially larger gains in challenging driving scenes where execution mismatch more strongly affects candidate evaluation.

</details>

Abstract: Actively tilting aerial robots can generate six-dimensional wrenches, but slow servo response can make a desired wrench infeasible during reconfiguration. This paper develops a control barrier function (CBF) allocator that preserves wrench feasibility under bounded servo-configuration mismatch. An exact equivalent-wrench identity connects the thrust required at nominal and actual configurations. For a nonsingular six-channel allocation map, inverse allocation reduces robust wrench containment to twelve thrust margins tightened by an ellipsoidal reserve. Their complete derivatives yield a six-variable servo-rate quadratic program, followed by differential thrust allocation for tracking. A conditional forward-invariance result establishes actual-configuration feasibility under reserve coverage, feasible initialization, and admissible CBF inputs. A complementary MuJoCo study evaluates a measured-angle implementation against geometric, augmented differential, and normalized differential allocation in circular and cartwheel maneuvers, with and without disturbances. The proposed implementation achieves lower position error in all four recorded scenarios, with an attitude tradeoff in circular tracking. During the disturbed cartwheel, the desired wrench is feasible at all 1751 stored samples of continuous rotation; baseline violation rates range from 60.8% to 100%. These comparisons evaluate sampled feasibility; validation of the nominal-trajectory certificate requires nominal-state and reserve-coverage records.

</details>


### [432] [Onboard Lightweight LLM Reasoning for Human-Aware Safe UAV Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22092)

Abstract: Aerial robots operating near people must treat a pedestrian, a parked car, and a wall differently, yet conventional perception--planning stacks reduce all of them to occupied geometry---a \emph{human-aware safety gap} in which category-specific clearance is unavailable to the planner and temporary occlusions remove pedestrian-specific constraints from its map. We present a modular, fully onboard pipeline that closes this gap by turning visual observations into context-dependent safety constraints for real-time UAV navigation. A shared BLIP encoder drives scene captioning and text-conditioned segmentation, which is fused with depth to localize obstacles in 3D. Obstacle-specific risk is inferred by a lightweight LLM (Qwen-2.5-0.5B) stabilized with retrieval-augmented generation and grammar-constrained decoding that restricts its output to a planner-compatible JSON schema. The LLM reasons once per object category rather than per frame: its verdicts are cached per class, so previously seen categories are resolved without inference and new ones trigger a fresh retrieval-grounded query; detections are additionally given persistent identities and held in the map across occlusions. The perception pipeline runs at 10\,Hz on a Jetson Orin NX, while fallback LLM queries take approximately 0.8\,s and remain off the control-critical path. In Gazebo, low-altitude urban navigation among pedestrians and vehicles shows a roughly $3\times$ reduction in collision events over a geometric planner, and semantics-aware landing among moving actors raises success from 34\% to 96\%. The 224-pixel encoder input limits reliable small-object perception at range; conservative pedestrian margins and short-term persistence mitigate this limitation during navigation.

</details>


### [433] [CF-Splat: Closed-Form Triangle Splatting with Mesh Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22108)
*Yixin Fang,Liang Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Splatting-based radiance-field representations have recently gained popularity in robotic perception, enabling high-quality novel-view synthesis (NVS) through explicit scene primitives. However, existing splatting methods typically rely on iterative nonlinear optimization that jointly optimizes millions of coupled primitive parameters, resulting in substantial computation time, peak GPU memory consumption, and susceptibility to local minima. In this paper, we propose CF-Splat, a novel closed-form triangle-splatting framework for efficient scene appearance estimation over a known mesh either pre-existing or provided by upstream mapping or reconstruction pipelines. Our key insight is that, by properly formulating the problem over the given mesh geometry and calibrated cameras, the rendered pixel colors can be expressed linearly with respect to the per-vertex spherical harmonics (SH) coefficients, allowing multi-view scene appearance estimation to be formulated as a global linear least-squares (LLS) problem without the need of iterative nonlinear optimization. To guarantee a well-posed solution under uneven multi-view observation coverage, we introduce adaptive SH regularization that constrains insufficiently supported high-order SH coefficients and yields a unique global minimizer. To ensure the scalability to large-scale scenes, we develop a triangle-structured matrix-free preconditioned conjugate-gradient solver that avoids explicitly constructing the large sparse normal system while producing the same LLS solution. Experiments on diverse real-world datasets demonstrate that CF-Splat achieves competitive or superior NVS quality compared with state-of-the-art splatting methods. While, CF-Splat requires approximately 27.5-378.0 times less computation time and 1.8 times lower peak GPU memory on average than 3DGS across scenes of different scales on a consumer-grade laptop.

</details>

Abstract: We propose Compliant GMT (C-GMT), a modular framework for full-body compliant responses in general motion tracking. As humanoid robots move into real world deployment, they not only need to perform any motion but also respond safely and robustly to interaction forces. Existing general motion tracking policies can imitate diverse motions but are trained to be stiff, leading to unexpected and unsafe behaviors under disturbance. Prior work in compliant humanoid control trades off compliance and generality. There exist examples of full-body compliant responses, but these are heavily curated to single-reference policies. On the other hand, general motion trackers (GMT) are trained to rigidly imitate massive motion capture datasets. Our key insight is to leverage the generality of pretrained GMTs for motion tracking while introducing compliance from a residual adapter that conditions on a history of proprioception. This enables our approach, C-GMT, to achieve both general motion tracking and whole-body compliant responses. Compared to state-of-the-art general motion trackers and single-motion compliant policies, C-GMT demonstrates competitive performance in both motion tracking accuracy and compliance adherence. We further demonstrate improved safety, robustness, and error tolerance in simulation and real-world G1 experiments.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models present a promising paradigm for general-purpose robot manipulation, but closed-loop deployment is often limited by inference efficiency: at each timestep, high-resolution, often multi-view images are encoded into hundreds of visual tokens that dominate transformer computation and latency. Token compression via attention is a natural solution but is challenging for VLA control because (1) decision-critical tokens are determined by the task, not generic visual saliency, and (2) the token budget varies substantially across steps, so static metrics and budgets waste computation or discard control-critical tokens. We introduce FastVLA, a training-free, task-aware, and temporally adaptive visual token compression framework. At each step, FastVLA scores token importance via task-to-vision attention and adaptively sets the compression rate from the importance-distribution uniformity, measured by an inverse-Simpson metric with online sliding-window calibration. We evaluate FastVLA on OpenVLA, OpenVLA-OFT, and CogACT across LIBERO and SIMPLER, and on a physical Franka Research 3 robot. In simulation, it reduces LLM-backbone FLOPs by 35-59\% and action latency by 9-32\% (a 1.09$\times$-1.46$\times$ speedup) while maintaining task success, achieving a comparable or better efficiency-performance trade-off than existing plug-and-play token compression/pruning/reuse baselines. On the real robot, it cuts end-to-end latency by 20\% (1.25$\times$ speedup) at comparable success. Code is available at https://github.com/jiuyixu25/FastVLA.

</details>

Abstract: The effective deployment of humanoid robots in practical tasks of interest will require a capability of sensing and adapting to variations in inertial parameters, as occurs during heavy tool use or when carrying significant payload. Such payloads constitute a significant proportion of the combined payload-robot system, the effects of which cannot be ignored by delegating to feedback control. This paper showcases a general solution to this problem by integrating a whole body model predictive controller (WB-MPC) with an extended Kalman filter (EKF) for online estimation of fully physically consistent time-varying inertial parameters. These estimates are fed through to the WB-MPC's full rigid body dynamics model, resulting in more accurate feedforward torques for task demands under external load. Additionally, a virtual reality (VR) interface is provided that enables an operator to provide task-specific references without regard to payload while delegating dynamic balance to the WB-MPC. The framework is validated with simulation and hardware experiments on a humanoid system.

</details>

Abstract: We introduce REKI, a 2.4B vision-language-action model (VLA) pretrained exclusively on public data that sets a new Pareto frontier between performance and model size: on LIBERO-Plus, RoboTwin Clean2Rand and RoboCasa365, it is outperformed only by models about twice its size. REKI consists of a vision-language model (VLM) followed by a flow-matching action expert, and is pretrained on a curated mixture of public robot, egocentric human and vision-language data. Additionally, we endow it with a memory that gives the policy access to its recent past at negligible cost: a few register tokens in the action expert summarise the output of the VLM at each frame and persist in the expert's cache. This memory improves success in simulation and on real robots, and makes oscillations between poses about four times rarer. REKI uses a simple model design and training paradigm, and is optimised to be efficient at training and inference. We release the full code across data curation, training, inference and evaluation, deployment, and all pretrained and finetuned model weights: https://github.com/kyutai-labs/reki.

### [438] [MOSS: Mode-Oriented Source Shaping for Robot Flow Matching Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22193)

Abstract: In robotic manipulation, the same observation often admits multiple feasible actions. Generative policies such as diffusion and flow matching naturally capture such multimodal behaviors, yet which mode is executed is left to random sampling and cannot be actively controlled. We propose Mode-Oriented Source Shaping (MOSS), which turns mode selection into an explicit choice of where generation starts. We first reveal, both empirically and theoretically, that deterministic flow transport induces mode-dependent structure in the source space. Under ideal deterministic ODE transport, distinct behavioral modes have distinct source-space preimages, while practical approximation errors can blur their boundaries due to imbalanced data, limited model capacity, and few inference steps. Motivated by this source-space structure, MOSS constructs semantically aligned mode-specific Gaussian sources that encode mode identity in the initial noise. A modality-strength estimator predicts the local degree of multimodality, which adaptively controls the geometry of the mode-specific sources. It contracts them toward a shared Gaussian where behaviors are similar and separates them near behavioral branching points. During deployment, the desired behavior is selected at inference simply by sampling from the corresponding component. Across 6 simulated and 3 real-world manipulation tasks, MOSS steers to the target mode up to 100% of the time with high task success, recovers minority modes at over 90% guidance rate under a 10:90 imbalance dataset, and surpasses mode-conditioned flow policy by 43.3 points of guidance rate on real robots.

</details>

Abstract: Propulsive drones are a promising new technology for generating high-resolution terrain models of the Moon. In future lunar missions, propulsive drones will complete a series of hops across the lunar surface while capturing images with downward-facing cameras. We present an autonomous motion planning framework that uses a vision-guided optimal control strategy for performing safe, efficient hops between specified waypoints. The drone plans parabolic hops between waypoints generated by a global-level planner; if a nominal waypoint is unsafe or unreachable, the drone then completes a diversion maneuver to safely land. To evaluate the validity of potential landing sites, we develop a custom semantic segmentation model for identifying obstacles from aerial images, and we introduce an approach for rapidly calculating reachable terrain. We then use model predictive control (MPC) to robustly track hop trajectories that guide the drone towards safe landing sites. We test the optimal hop planner in a high-fidelity lunar simulation environment. Our results demonstrate that our unified motion planning framework yields safe, fuel-efficient hop trajectories which can be tracked rapidly and with low error via MPC.

</details>


### [440] [Asking Smarter Questions: Targeted Active Preference Learning for Underspecified Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22285)

Abstract: Reward learning typically assumes the human's input covers everything that matters for the task. In practice, humans can only attend to so much at once, so their teaching naturally captures some features---or aspects of the task---better than others. Prior work has shown how the robot can identify which features were poorly supervised directly from its teaching data. But the current solution is to ask the human to try again, putting the burden on someone who already struggled to convey those features. We propose shifting this burden to the robot. Our key insight is that the same information that tells the robot what it doesn't know can help it ask better questions. Preference queries offer a low-effort channel for resolving this uncertainty, but only if the robot knows where to direct them and the human knows what to attend to when answering. We act on this insight in two ways: we build an informed prior that targets preference queries at the features that need resolving, and we accompany each query with an explanation that focuses the human's attention on exactly those features. In simulation, the informed prior beats the standard active preference learning baseline by up to $30.8\%$ in the low-query regime, where reducing human effort matters most. In a user study, the informed prior and explanations each significantly improve reward recovery and amplify each other.

</details>


### [441] [Mem4Act: Learning Effective Latent Memory for Long-Horizon Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22323)
*Yun Zhang,Zhiyu Huang,Tianhui Cai,Chen Tang,Wei Wang,Jiaqi Ma*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: 解析错误

Abstract: Long-horizon robotic manipulation fundamentally requires memory: agents must retain task-relevant information from past observations and interactions and use it to guide future actions. Existing approaches typically preserve raw historical observations, compressed visual tokens, or language summaries, but these representations either expose the action policy to increasingly long histories or risk discarding task-relevant temporal, semantic, and perceptual information. We introduce Mem4Act, a framework for learning effective latent memory as a fixed-size representation explicitly optimized for long-horizon robotic control. Mem4Act addresses three coupled challenges: how to efficiently cover long histories, what information memory should retain, and how to ensure the policy actually relies on memory. To this end, it organizes history via multi-granularity temporal aggregation, preserves complementary semantic and perceptual information through question-answering supervision and key-frame DINO feature reconstruction, and employs memory-first two-stage training to encourage greater use of memory by the action policy. The resulting memory captures both high-level task state and fine-grained perceptual evidence while providing a bounded interface to the action policy. Across RoboMME, RMBench, and four real-world manipulation tasks, Mem4Act achieves state-of-the-art performance on memory-dependent control, with success rates of 49.88%, 61.0%, and 81.5%, respectively. Extensive ablations further demonstrate the importance of temporal aggregation, complementary memory supervision, and memory-first training.

</details>

Abstract: Harsh and rapidly varying orbital illumination introduces failure modes during spacecraft inspection. Existing planners rely on line-of-sight inspection metrics which perform occlusion checks against a known target and conflate geometric visibility with task-relevant information. These metrics overlook illumination effects including eclipse, self-shadowing, and specular reflection from spacecraft materials and tend to declare components as inspected despite the corresponding images lacking sufficient information. We propose an inspection-planning framework that replaces geometric visibility with a learned measure of camera information derived from a classifier variational autoencoder (VAE) that operates directly on the captured image. The encoder model is trained to classify spacecraft surface components from rendered patches, and its total latent variance is used to learn the varying illumination of said components, replacing binary visibility checks with a continuous, image-grounded quantity. We evaluate the approach by implementing a Guidance, Navigation, and Control architecture in a photorealistic physics simulator, and vary lighting and viewing geometries with multiple orbits. We show that, although all approaches successfully maximize their own objective, our approach results in better illumination: a mean brightness of $0.57$, with $81.6\%$ of observations above $0.25$, compared with $0.02$ ($3.0\%$) for geometric planning with an illumination band and $0.04$ ($6.0\%$) for purely geometric planning.

</details>

Abstract: How can robots autonomously collect the contact-rich experience needed to learn dexterous manipulation? We present DSP: Dexterity from supervised play, a framework in which a robot learns a play policy from minutes of human play through behavior cloning, then deploys it autonomously to collect hours of interaction data to train a world model. During inference, the same play policy then guides model-predictive control (MPC) by proposing candidate action sequences for evaluation under the learned world model. To accelerate inference, we also introduce a method for distilling a closed-loop, goal-conditioned policy through self-play within the learned world model. We evaluate this framework on underactuated pushing and any-to-any in-hand pose reorientation with a multifingered hand, in simulation and real world. In real world experiment, our method outperforms hindsight behavior cloning by 69.6% in success rate and achieves comparable success to reinforcement learning with 490 times less interaction data. These results demonstrate that supervised play can autonomously collect interaction data, guide model-based planning, and enable policy learning without any task-specific demonstrations or sim-to-real transfer.

</details>

Abstract: Robotic harvesting requires precise manipulation to ensure accurate positioning of the end-effector, especially in a constraint workspace. This study introduces the Path-Safe Approach Proximal Policy Optimization (PSA-PPO), a target-conditioned local reaching framework designed for robotic harvesting of nutrient film technique (NFT)-hydroponic lettuce in greenhouses. The proposed PSA-PPO preserves the standard clipped PPO objective while introducing a harvesting-oriented policy formulation that integrates target-conditioned observations, corridor-aware spatial constraints, trajectory-history-based safety encoding, and task-specific success criteria for constrained robotic manipulation. PSAPPO was trained entirely in Isaac Lab and successfully transferred to a physical UR10e robotic arm without any real-world policy fine-tuning. In a controlled physical benchmark against 150 scheduled targets, PSA-PPO achieved the highest reaching rate of 94.7%, outperforming Cartesian+TOTG (86.0%), RRTConnect (88.0%), and RRT* (80.0%), while reducing median RMS jerk by 39.8 to 73.0% relative to the classical planners during the final approach segment. A formulation ablation evaluated over 200 simulation targets demonstrates that path-safety and side-corridor shaping are the principal drivers of harvesting-compatible performance: removing either term substantially degrades strict success even when terminal endpoint accuracy is preserved or improved, confirming that optimization toward the endpoint alone is an insufficient objective for constrained crop-proximal manipulation. Finally, PSA-PPO was integrated with an RGB-D vision module and an end-effector and evaluated as a complete greenhouse harvesting system across 144 lettuce harvesting attempts, achieving 94.4% vision-guided target reaching and 86.1% complete harvesting success.

</details>

Abstract: Generalist robot policies benefit from large-scale visual pretraining but often struggle with contact-rich dexterous manipulation, where force, compliance, and incipient slip are hard to observe from images. We present Spark-0, a spatially aligned vision-tactile-language-action (VTLA) model that uses touch in two complementary ways. During pretraining, image-space contact maps derived from tactile gloves, together with features from a frozen 3D geometry teacher, supervise the visual tokens; these objectives are removed at inference. In parallel, a tactile pathway encodes regional force histories and 3D taxel states to condition the action expert. A single checkpoint therefore supports both vision-only and tactile-conditioned deployment. To scale this supervision, we build HumanTouch, a 1,000-hour dataset of full-hand tactile human manipulation, and pretrain Spark-0 on it together with egocentric video, robot data, and simulation. We also release SparkArena, which pairs vision-only simulated tasks with tactile-conditioned real-robot tasks on one dexterous embodiment. Spark-0 achieves the highest mean success rate on both SparkArena tracks and ranks first on all eight aggregate metrics of the Ego Humanoid Manipulation Benchmark. Compared with vision-only pretraining on the same data and compute with touch discarded, retaining the touch of 1,000 hours of HumanTouch raises vision-only success on Ego Humanoid from 52.5% to 71.7%, and success on SparkArena-Real, where touch is also used at deployment, from 44.0% to 52.0%, indicating that touch serves not only as a policy input but also as scalable supervision for dexterous visuomotor pretraining. The Website is available at: https://xsparkai.com/Spark-0.

</details>

Abstract: Generative simulation can automate robot environment construction, but physically valid environments are not necessarily useful for training. The challenge is to identify environments that address a policy's failures and supervision that helps it overcome them. We introduce CoDA, a framework in which a simulation data engine and an action policy co-evolve through a shared heterogeneous memory of environment construction, policy failures, and learning progress. The memory compiles validated diagnoses from a vision-language critic into generation programs that recreate difficulties and training programs that help the policy overcome them. We further propose Text2potential to convert these diagnoses into staged potentials for action-chunk value learning, providing dense supervision for policy updates while preserving the task's optimal policy under standard shaping assumptions. Measured learning progress guides further generation and program refinement. Across four manipulation task families, CoDA improves out-of-distribution success from 5.3% after supervised fine-tuning to 27.6%, exceeding the strongest closed-loop baseline by 9.5 percentage points with the same generation budget. On LIBERO-Pro, 15 rounds of generation and training raise success from 25% to 84% under position perturbations and from 1% to 76% under instruction perturbations.

</details>


### [447] [T2-z: Test-Time Optimization of Environment Latents for Robotic World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22364)

Abstract: Predicting how the world responds to an action requires more than what can be seen: robots must account for environmental factors that are neither directly observable nor explicitly specified, from physical properties such as friction and mass to human-defined environmental rules. We introduce T2-Z for test-time optimization of environment latents, which augments a robotic world model with a learnable environment latent to condition world predictions. This compact latent captures underlying environmental variation without supervision of what environmental factors exist or what values they take. During training, T2-Z alternates between two stages: a world-model optimization stage that fixes the latents and learns general action-conditioned dynamics across environments; and an environment-latent optimization stage that freezes the world model and optimizes the conditioning latents, allowing latent structure to emerge purely through world prediction. At test time, the world model remains frozen and a newly initialized environment latent is optimized from one or more test-time interactions, adapting the model to previously unseen environmental configurations. Across 7 simulated settings and 4 real-world robotic tasks, T2-Z reduces object prediction error metric from 15.20 to 7.00 and raises action success from 33.73% to 72.96%. Its emergent latents capture structured representations of individual and compounded factors across diverse visual scenes. Ablations further show that T2-Z can efficiently infer environment latents from as little as a single test-time interaction trajectory.

</details>

Abstract: Real-world post-training of generalist robot policies faces two challenges: partial observability makes action selection ambiguous, while offline post-training methods delay feedback from deployment experience. We present Value as Memory (VaM), a framework that addresses these challenges through value caching and online real-world reinforcement learning (RL). Value caching conditions actions on recent value predictions as task-progress memory, while online updates enable timely policy improvement by combining offline demonstrations, off-policy interventions, and on-policy rollouts for sample-efficient adaptation. As a result, VaM transitions from offline RL warmup to online autonomous learning with sparse rewards. Across long-horizon tasks involving deformable objects and contact-rich manipulation, VaM yields a mean absolute success-rate gain of $29.4\%$ with value caching, while achieving a $32.8\%$ gain with online autonomous learning over matched offline training. The evaluated online schedule performs $8\times$ as many policy iterations under the same gradient-step budget, with more than $2\times$ lower recorded time per gradient step with concurrent data collection and training.

</details>


### [449] [LIBERO-PeRM: Benchmarking Personalized Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22372)

Abstract: Personalization is essential for general-purpose robots to transition into household environments and achieve true practicality. While general robotic manipulation has been extensively studied, personalized robotic manipulation (PeRM) lacks a controlled benchmark for separating task completion from preference satisfaction. We introduce LIBERO-PeRM, a benchmark for preference learning in embodied agents. LIBERO-PeRM studies five questions: 1) how policies follow preferences in zero-shot settings; 2) how they learn single preferences from demonstrations; 3) how efficiently they acquire preferences as demonstrations increase; 4) how learned preferences generalize across layouts and tasks; and 5) how policies compose preferences and behave under conflicts. We propose a controlled formulation and taxonomy of observable preferences in simulation, covering motion, skill, and user-profile choices. This formulation is restricted to language-expressible preferences that can be instantiated in LIBERO without changing the task goal, evaluated automatically, and reused across tasks. Building on LIBERO, our procedural pipeline generates paired demonstrations with and without preferences. We create five task suites (312 tasks) with high-quality demonstrations, rich metadata, and preference-satisfaction metrics. Experiments show that current VLA policies struggle with zero-shot personalization, while SFT makes preferences learnable. Mixed training achieves better no-preference retention than sequential adaptation and improves performance with contextual prompts compared with preference-only training, but can weaken motion-preference learning; preference generalization, decomposition, and conflict handling remain difficult. The code, datasets, and demos can be found on the project website: https://libero-perm.github.io/.

</details>

Abstract: O-RAN enables the deployment of heterogeneous control and provisioning algorithms through open interfaces and programmable intelligent controllers. However, most existing intelligent O-RAN solutions rely on a single optimization strategy designed for a fixed operational objective, limiting their ability to adapt when network conditions or operator priorities change. This paper proposes an objective-aware policy orchestration framework that selects the most appropriate provisioning policy for each control window based on the current network context and operator objective. The framework orchestrates a portfolio of heterogeneous provisioning experts using contextual-bandit learning. Two policy selectors, Linear Thompson Sampling and a Bootstrap Tree ensemble, are evaluated against static provisioning policies and a deterministic rule-based orchestrator. Experimental results show that learning-based policy orchestration can effectively adapt policy selection to varying operating conditions, improving overall objective-aware performance while preserving the modularity of existing provisioning algorithms.

</details>

Abstract: This paper proposes an integer-free method for control synthesis of nonlinear discrete-time systems under finite-horizon Signal Temporal Logic (STL) constraints. Instead of introducing integer variables, we consider the discrete choices of the specification in a ranked order and solve a standard nonlinear program for each selected choice until a satisfying trajectory is found. We first define implicants as sets of timed atomic constraints whose joint satisfaction is sufficient to satisfy the specification. A reference trajectory obtained from a smooth approximation of the STL robustness is used to rank these implicants. Fixing one implicant turns the problem into a standard nonlinear program with no auxiliary weights or bilinear terms. If the solution does not satisfy the specification, the next implicant will be considered. We prove soundness and completeness of our method relative to the nonlinear programming solver. Numerical evaluation on nonlinear multi-agent systems shows that our proposed method identifies satisfying trajectories where the exact continuous reformulation fails, and moreover, it is faster than a mixed-integer formulation of the same specification.

</details>


### [452] [Physical AI: A Survey of Models, Learning, Systems, and Open Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21171)
*Bin Gao,Anuj Gautam,Aritra Bagchi,Yunkai Liang,Wentao Li,Ronghui Li,Zhi Zhou,Yancan Mao,Xiao Yan,Xin He,Qingyun Zou,Wenshuo Yue,Dan Chen,Zhanglu Yan,Fei Xu,Huize Li,Weijie Wang,Tao Ouyang,Tulika Mitra*

Abstract: Physical AI aims to enable intelligent systems to perceive, reason, act, and learn through interaction with the physical world. Unlike purely digital intelligence, physical agents must operate under partial observability, uncertain dynamics, embodiment constraints, and real-time requirements, while their actions continuously reshape the environment and subsequent observations. Recent advances in foundation models and robot learning have expanded the capabilities of physical agents, yet achieving reliable autonomy requires more than improvements in individual models. This survey presents a comprehensive, system-level review of Physical AI, organized around the closed-loop process of physical interaction. We examine the foundations and recent developments in task representations and memory, predictive and action-generating models, data and learning methods, embodiment and control, computing systems, evaluation, and safety. Beyond reviewing individual advances, we investigate how these components interact and collectively shape the capabilities and limitations of Physical AI systems. Building on this integrated perspective, we identify fundamental challenges in generalization, resource-efficient learning and execution, reliable state tracking, safety, recovery, and continual adaptation. We argue that advancing Physical AI requires moving beyond isolated model performance toward integrated systems capable of robust, efficient, safe, and autonomous physical interaction under changing real-world conditions.

</details>

Abstract: In this paper we study the dynamical properties induced by safety filters based on control barrier functions (CBFs). These are obtained by minimally modifying a nominal controller with the goal of rendering it safe. We consider the simple setting of a planar linear plant with a stable linear nominal controller and an affine constraint. We show that even in this simple setting, the filtered system can oscillate or diverge while remaining safe. We fully characterize the parameters for which the origin is globally (exponentially or asymptotically) stable. For all other parameters, the filter creates a saddle on the constraint boundary, and we characterize the region of attraction of the origin. For underdamped nominal dynamics, its boundary is an unstable periodic orbit, a homoclinic loop, or the saddle's stable manifold, depending on whether the CBF gain is below, at, or above a homoclinic transition value. A vehicle sideslip example and two three-dimensional examples illustrate the results and what changes beyond planar dynamics.

</details>

Abstract: Vertiports must accommodate electric vertical takeoff and landing (eVTOL) aircraft with different climb capabilities and crosswind tolerances. However, conventional runway based departure criteria do not exploit the directional freedom of vertical takeoff. This paper proposes a framework that evaluates every departure azimuth and identifies usable and preferred departure directions for each climb capability. Each azimuth is evaluated by its obstacle clearance from surrounding urban buildings and its wind operability based on ten years of wind observations. Three climb angle layers reflect differences in climb capability among aircraft. Four weighting scenarios between obstacles and wind reveal how operational priority affects the preferred departure direction. In an application to Suseo station and the Jamsil heliport in Seoul, steeper climbs widened the usable azimuths from 8.9 to 34.4% and from 66.1 to 83.6%, respectively. By relaxing obstacle constraints, steeper climbs also shifted the preferred direction toward the prevailing wind at lower wind weights. These results show that vertiport departure paths should be planned as a set of candidate directions matched to the obstacles and wind of the site and the climb capability of the aircraft, rather than as a single runway style path.

</details>

Abstract: This paper considers the problem of policy-gradient transfer from a simulator to a physical plant when both are controlled through the same fixed state estimator. The plant and the simulator are partially observed nonlinear systems with additive Gaussian noise of common known covariance whose dynamics and observation models differ, and the controller is a squashed Gaussian policy with uniformly bounded latent mean and first two parameter derivatives. We derive a bound on the difference between the plant and simulator policy gradients that is linear in the noise-weighted model error and requires no estimator-accuracy guarantee, because the shared estimator is a common post-processing of the measurements and the data-processing inequality compares the trajectory laws without an estimation-error term. Based on this bound, we derive an occupancy-weighted refinement, a sufficient condition for the simulator gradient to be an ascent direction for the plant, and a bound on the expected squared plant gradient at a random iterate of a finite simulator-training run with optimization, truncation and model-error terms. Simulation results on a scalar system and a pendulum are presented to validate the theoretical results.

</details>


### [456] [Scaling up statistical model checking of cyber-physical systems via algorithm ensemble and parallel simulations over HPC infrastructures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21772)

Abstract: Model-based formal verification of industry-relevant Cyber-Physical Systems (CPSs) is often a computationally prohibitive task. In most cases, the complexity of the models precludes any prospect of symbolic analysis, leaving numerical simulation as the only viable option. Unfortunately, exhaustive simulation of a CPS model over the entire set of plausible operational scenarios is rarely possible in practice, and alternative strategies such as Statistical Model Checking (SMC) must be used instead. In this article, we show that the number of model simulations (samples) required by SMC techniques to converge can be significantly reduced by considering multiple (an ensemble of) Adaptive Stopping Algorithms (SAs) at once, and that the simulations themselves (by far the most expensive step of the entire workload) can be efficiently sped up by exploiting massively parallel platforms. With three industry-scale CPS models, we experimentally show that the use of an ensemble of two state-of-the-art SAs (AA and EBGStop) may require dozens of millions fewer samples when compared to running a single algorithm, with reductions in sample size of up to 78%. Furthermore, we show that our implementation, by massively parallelizing system model simulations on a HPC infrastructure, yields speed-ups for the completion time of the verification tasks which are practically linear with respect to the number of computational nodes, thus achieving an efficiency of virtually 100%, even on very large platforms. This makes it possible to complete tasks of model-based SMC verification for complex CPSs in a matter of hours or days, whereas a naïve sequential execution would require from months to many years.

</details>

Abstract: This paper studies optimal energy conversion in port-Hamiltonian systems. We analyze state- and control-constrained finite-horizon optimal control problems that maximize energy conversion efficiencies. Under suitable reachability conditions, we show that the asymptotic optimal efficiencies exist and coincide with the corresponding optimal cyclic efficiencies. For a class of port-Hamiltonian systems describing coupled storage-conversion components, we further derive intrinsic limits on achievable energy conversion and establish a generalized Carnot-type efficiency bound. Our results provide an optimal-control framework connecting asymptotic optimal operation, cyclic operation, and fundamental efficiency limits for port-Hamiltonian systems.

</details>

Abstract: Online identification of frequency response functions is increasingly relevant for control and monitoring of high-performance systems whose dynamics may change during operation. In modern machines, however, continuously transmitting all locally acquired measurements to a central unit for identification can impose substantial communication demands, particularly as sensing rates and the number of subsystems increase. This paper develops an event-triggered data-selection approach for remote frequency-domain identification, in which periodically acquired measurements are evaluated locally and transmitted only when relevant. The selection mechanism combines information about the current estimation uncertainty with evidence of model mismatch, reducing communication while preserving identification accuracy and responsiveness to changes in the dynamics.

</details>

Abstract: Mine accidents can damage the wired backbone supporting underground wireless networks, forcing rescue teams to deploy temporary mesh nodes to maintain communication with the surface. Although higher carrier frequencies can support bandwidth-intensive applications such as live video, multimode propagation within mine tunnels produces severe, spatially localized fading. Movable antennas can mitigate these fades by adjusting antenna position; however, moving all antennas on a multi-link relay node simultaneously may improve one link at the expense of another. This paper introduces QuadBeam, a compact mesh node antenna system comprising four independently positionable 2.4~GHz patch antennas mounted on the side faces of a central hub, together with a 900~MHz monopole for backup voice communication. A preliminary prototype incorporates one motor-driven patch mount and three manually adjustable mounts. Bench measurements show interelement coupling below $-46$~dB between patches on adjacent faces. Measurements at five carrier frequencies from 2.40~GHz to 2.48~GHz in the Edgar Experimental Mine reveal 19~dB to 26~dB received-power variations across transmitter and receiver separations of 8.3~m to 18.3~m. Adjusting the receiver height over approximately 20~cm yields received-power improvements of up to 22~dB. Repositioning neighboring patches and removing or reinstalling the monopole produce no noticeable change in the active link. These results demonstrate the potential of independently positioned antennas to alleviate spatial fading in underground mesh communications.

</details>

Abstract: Safe multi-agent coordination typically assumes what real-world, deployed, resource-constrained robots cannot provide: continuous, perfect state sharing. Robust control strategies must be complemented with robust communication strategies to deliver resilient deployments. In this work, we propose a unified control/communication strategy that provides safe control to guarantee inter-agent safety under intermittent communication and demonstrate its effectiveness in a pursuit-evasion scenario. Each agent computes the reachable set of neighboring agents based on the last state information received, then solves for a safe control input based on control barrier functions to ensure agents avoid collision. We prove that inter-agent collision avoidance is guaranteed provided communication staleness remains bounded. As the reachable set inflates between state transmissions, each agent reasons about when to preemptively transmit its state information to neighboring agents based on novel metrics characterizing uncertainty risk and kinematic risk. We implement and simulate a variety of pursuit-evasion scenarios and show that preemptive communication reduces messages by 77.5% compared to periodic and event-triggered baselines with zero collisions.

</details>

*Federico Cecati,Yang Wu,Florian Dörfler,Marco Liserre,Xiongfei Wang,Elisabetta Tedeschi*

Abstract: Stability issues in converter-dominated grids have a multifold nature, where static, small-signal and large-signal phenomena can influence each other. Capturing the interplay among these different stability aspects requires a rethinking of conventional modeling and analysis tools for power systems. As a further complication, Intellectual Property protection among manufacturers and system operators makes detailed converters and grid models unattainable. This motivates the use of data-driven approaches for stability analysis and control, nevertheless achieving an insightful representation of static, small-signal and large-signal phenomena from data still leaves many open questions. This paper discusses model-based and data-driven methodologies for stability analysis of converter-dominated grids. The instability mechanisms of static, small-signal and large-signal phenomena are conceptually explained, and the capability of different modeling and analysis tools to investigate them is examined. The effectiveness of data-driven methodologies to analyze instability phenomena in their multifold nature is debated, and the challenges on data completeness, model generalization under untrained conditions, physical interpretability, and uncertainty awareness are discussed.

</details>

Abstract: Effective medical simulators necessitate realistic haptic rendering of biological tissues that exhibit viscoelastic material properties, such as creep and stress relaxation. Fractional-order models provide an effective means of describing intrinsically time-dependent viscoelastic dynamics with few parameters, as they naturally capture memory effects. However, due to the unintuitive, frequency-dependent coupling among the order of the fractional element and other parameters, determining appropriate parameter values for fractional-order models that yield high perceived realism remains a significant challenge. In this study, we propose a systematic means of determining the parameters of fractional-order viscoelastic models that optimizes the perceived realism of haptic rendering across general populations. First, we demonstrate that the parameters of fractional-order models can be effectively optimized through active learning, using qualitative feedback-based human-in-the-loop (HiL) optimization, to ensure consistently high realism ratings for each individual. Second, we propose a rigorous method to combine HiL optimization results into an aggregate perceptual map trained on the entire dataset, and demonstrate how to select population-level optimal parameters from this representation that are broadly perceived as realistic across general populations. Finally, we provide evidence of the effectiveness of the generalized fractional-order viscoelastic model parameters for three viscoelastic materials by characterizing their perceived realism through human-subject experiments. Overall, generalized fractional-order viscoelastic models established through the proposed HiL optimization and aggregation approach possess the potential to significantly improve the sim-to-real transition performance of medical training simulators.

</details>

*Hassan Yazdanian,Henrik Mäkinen*

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Multi-contrast X-ray computed tomography (CT) combines attenuation contrast with phase and dark-field contrast, which are sensitive to refraction and ultra-small-angle scattering, respectively. Common implementations record several intensity measurements per projection angle by phase or mask stepping, which lengthens acquisition and, at a fixed exposure per measurement, raises the dose. Limited-data acquisition via sparse angular sampling or reduced stepping lowers these costs but makes the data less informative, calling for prior information and uncertainty quantification. We study joint Bayesian reconstruction of attenuation, phase, and dark-field images from separately retrieved sinograms. A joint total variation term in the prior couples edge locations across the images but not their values. Posterior sampling with the No-U-Turn Sampler complements maximum a posteriori estimates with pixel-wise uncertainty. We compare this joint prior with channel-wise total variation alone on synthetic and experimental Talbot-Lau interferometry data under full, sparse-angle, sparse-step, and combined acquisition (up to 24 times fewer exposures). Both maximum a posteriori reconstructions suppressed undersampling artifacts, with synthetic relative errors 1.6-11 times lower than those of filtered backprojection. The joint prior gave modest synthetic accuracy gains, a cleaner experimental dark-field background, 23-30 % lower mean dark-field posterior standard deviation, and 4-38% higher median effective sample size in experimental limited-data runs. In the synthetic study, posterior standard deviations matched or exceeded the errors in flat regions but underestimated them at boundaries, especially in phase. Experimental phase chains mixed slowly under angular undersampling. The formulation also applies to edge illumination with adapted forward and noise models.

</details>
